<p><b>Alexander Frederick Douglas-Home, Baron Home of the Hirsel</b> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:100%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Knight_Companion_of_the_Order_of_the_Thistle" class="mw-redirect" title="Knight Companion of the Order of the Thistle">KT</a><span style="font-size:14px;">&#44;</span>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Privy_Council_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Privy Council of the United Kingdom">PC</a></span> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;h&#39; in &#39;hi&#39;">h</span><span title="/juː/: &#39;u&#39; in &#39;cute&#39;">juː</span><span title="&#39;m&#39; in &#39;my&#39;">m</span></span>/</a></span></span>; 2 July 1903&#160;– 9 October 1995), styled as <b>Lord Dunglass</b> between 1918 and 1951 and the <b>Earl of Home</b> from 1951 until 1963, was a British <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK)" title="Conservative Party (UK)">Conservative</a> politician who served as <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Prime Minister of the United Kingdom">Prime Minister of the United Kingdom</a> from 1963 to 1964. He is notable for being the last Prime Minister to hold office while being a member of the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Lords" title="House of Lords">House of Lords</a>, before renouncing his peerage and taking up a seat in the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="House of Commons of the United Kingdom">House of Commons</a> for the remainder of his premiership. His reputation, however, rests more on his two stints as <a href="/wiki/Foreign_Secretary" title="Foreign Secretary">Foreign Secretary</a> than on his brief premiership.
</p><p>Within six years of first entering the House of Commons in 1931, Douglas-Home (then called by the <a href="/wiki/Courtesy_titles_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="Courtesy titles in the United Kingdom">courtesy title</a> Lord Dunglass) became a parliamentary aide to <a href="/wiki/Neville_Chamberlain" title="Neville Chamberlain">Neville Chamberlain</a>, witnessing at first hand Chamberlain's efforts as Prime Minister to preserve peace through <a href="/wiki/Appeasement#Conduct_of_appeasement,_1937–1939" title="Appeasement">appeasement</a> in the two years before the outbreak of the Second World War. In 1940 Dunglass was diagnosed with spinal <a href="/wiki/Tuberculosis" title="Tuberculosis">tuberculosis</a> and was immobilised for two years. By the later stages of the war he had recovered enough to resume his political career, but he lost his seat in the <a href="/wiki/1945_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1945 United Kingdom general election">general election of 1945</a>. He regained it in 1950, but the following year he left the Commons when, on the death of his father, he inherited the earldom of Home and thereby became a member of the House of Lords. Under the premierships of <a href="/wiki/Winston_Churchill" title="Winston Churchill">Winston Churchill</a>, <a href="/wiki/Anthony_Eden" title="Anthony Eden">Anthony Eden</a> and <a href="/wiki/Harold_Macmillan" title="Harold Macmillan">Harold Macmillan</a> he was appointed to a series of increasingly senior posts, including <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_House_of_Lords" title="Leader of the House of Lords">Leader of the House of Lords</a> and <a href="/wiki/Foreign_Secretary" title="Foreign Secretary">Foreign Secretary</a>. In the latter post, which he held from 1960 to 1963, he supported United States resolve in the <a href="/wiki/Cuban_Missile_Crisis" title="Cuban Missile Crisis">Cuban Missile Crisis</a> and in August 1963 was the United Kingdom's signatory to the <a href="/wiki/Partial_Nuclear_Test_Ban_Treaty" title="Partial Nuclear Test Ban Treaty">Partial Nuclear Test Ban Treaty</a>.
</p><p>In October 1963 Macmillan was taken ill and resigned as Prime Minister. Home was chosen to succeed him. By the 1960s it had become generally considered unacceptable for a Prime Minister to sit in the House of Lords; Home renounced his earldom and successfully stood for election to the House of Commons. The manner of his appointment was controversial, and two of Macmillan's cabinet ministers refused to take office under him. He was criticised by the <a href="/wiki/Labour_Party_(UK)" title="Labour Party (UK)">Labour Party</a> as an aristocrat, out of touch with the problems of ordinary families, and he came over stiffly in television interviews, by contrast with the Labour leader, <a href="/wiki/Harold_Wilson" title="Harold Wilson">Harold Wilson</a>. The Conservative Party, in power since 1951, had lost standing as a result of the <a href="/wiki/Profumo_affair" title="Profumo affair">Profumo affair</a>, a 1963 sex scandal involving a defence minister, and at the time of Home's appointment as Prime Minister it seemed headed for heavy electoral defeat. Home's premiership was the second briefest of the twentieth century, lasting two days short of a year. Among the legislation passed under his government was the abolition of <a href="/wiki/Resale_price_maintenance" title="Resale price maintenance">resale price maintenance</a>, bringing costs down for the consumer against the interests of producers of food and other commodities.
</p><p>After a narrow defeat in <a href="/wiki/1964_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1964 United Kingdom general election">the general election of 1964</a>, Douglas-Home resigned the leadership of his party, after having instituted a new and less secretive method of electing the party leader. From 1970 to 1974 he served in the cabinet of <a href="/wiki/Edward_Heath" title="Edward Heath">Edward Heath</a> as Secretary of State at the <a href="/wiki/Foreign_and_Commonwealth_Office" class="mw-redirect" title="Foreign and Commonwealth Office">Foreign and Commonwealth Office</a>; this was an expanded version of the post of Foreign Secretary, which he had held earlier. After the defeat of the Heath government in 1974, he returned to the House of Lords as a <a href="/wiki/Life_peer" title="Life peer">life peer</a>, and retired from front-line politics.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life_and_education">Early life and education</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alec_Douglas-Home&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life and education">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Douglas-Home was born on 2 July 1903 at 28 <a href="/wiki/South_Street,_Mayfair" title="South Street, Mayfair">South Street</a> in <a href="/wiki/Mayfair" title="Mayfair">Mayfair</a>, London, the first of seven children of <a href="/wiki/Charles_Douglas-Home,_13th_Earl_of_Home" title="Charles Douglas-Home, 13th Earl of Home">Lord Dunglass</a> (the eldest son of the <a href="/wiki/Charles_Douglas-Home,_12th_Earl_of_Home" title="Charles Douglas-Home, 12th Earl of Home">12th Earl of Home</a>) and of his wife, the Lady Lilian Lambton (daughter of the <a href="/wiki/Frederick_Lambton,_4th_Earl_of_Durham" title="Frederick Lambton, 4th Earl of Durham">4th Earl of Durham</a>). The boy's first name was customarily abbreviated to "Alec".<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> Among the couple's younger children was the playwright <a href="/wiki/William_Douglas-Home" class="mw-redirect" title="William Douglas-Home">William Douglas-Home</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-timesobit_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-timesobit-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1918 the 12th Earl of Home died; Dunglass succeeded him in the earldom, and the <a href="/wiki/Courtesy_titles_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="Courtesy titles in the United Kingdom">courtesy title</a> passed to his son, Alec Douglas-Home, who was styled Lord Dunglass until 1951.<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> The young Lord Dunglass was educated at <a href="/wiki/Ludgrove_School" title="Ludgrove School">Ludgrove School</a>, followed by <a href="/wiki/Eton_College" title="Eton College">Eton College</a>. At Eton his contemporaries included <a href="/wiki/Cyril_Connolly" title="Cyril Connolly">Cyril Connolly</a>, who later described him as:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>[A] votary of the esoteric Eton religion, the kind of graceful, tolerant, sleepy boy who is showered with favours and crowned with all the laurels, who is liked by the masters and admired by the boys without any apparent exertion on his part, without experiencing the ill-effects of success himself or arousing the pangs of envy in others. In the 18th century he would have become prime minister before he was 30. As it was, he appeared honourably ineligible for the struggle of life.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>After Eton, Dunglass went to <a href="/wiki/Christ_Church,_Oxford" title="Christ Church, Oxford">Christ Church, Oxford</a>, where he graduated with a <a href="/wiki/British_undergraduate_degree_classification" title="British undergraduate degree classification">third-class honours</a> <a href="/wiki/Bachelor_of_Arts" title="Bachelor of Arts">BA degree</a> in <a href="/wiki/Modern_History" class="mw-redirect" title="Modern History">Modern History</a> in 1925.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Dunglass-Eton-and-Harrow-Match-1921.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="young man in cricket whites at the wicket" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/2/24/Dunglass-Eton-and-Harrow-Match-1921.jpg/150px-Dunglass-Eton-and-Harrow-Match-1921.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="321" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/2/24/Dunglass-Eton-and-Harrow-Match-1921.jpg/225px-Dunglass-Eton-and-Harrow-Match-1921.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/2/24/Dunglass-Eton-and-Harrow-Match-1921.jpg 2x" data-file-width="233" data-file-height="498" /></a><figcaption>As a member of the Eton XI, 1921</figcaption></figure>
<p>Dunglass was a talented sportsman. In addition to representing Eton at <a href="/wiki/Fives" title="Fives">fives</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> he was a capable cricketer at school, club and county level, and was unique among British prime ministers in having played <a href="/wiki/First-class_cricket" title="First-class cricket">first-class cricket</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-cricinfo_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cricinfo-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> Coached by <a href="/wiki/George_Hirst" title="George Hirst">George Hirst</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> he became in <a href="/wiki/Wisden_Cricketers%27_Almanack" title="Wisden Cricketers&#39; Almanack"><i>Wisden</i></a>'s phrase "a useful member of the Eton&#160;XI"<sup id="cite_ref-cricinfo_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cricinfo-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> that included <a href="/wiki/Percy_Lawrie" title="Percy Lawrie">Percy Lawrie</a> and <a href="/wiki/Gubby_Allen" title="Gubby Allen">Gubby Allen</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> Wisden observed, "In the rain-affected Eton-Harrow match of 1922 he scored 66, despite being hindered by a saturated outfield, and then took 4 for 37 with his medium-paced out-swingers".<sup id="cite_ref-cricinfo_7-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cricinfo-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> At first-class level he represented the <a href="/wiki/Oxford_University_Cricket_Club" title="Oxford University Cricket Club">Oxford University Cricket Club</a>, <a href="/wiki/Middlesex_County_Cricket_Club" title="Middlesex County Cricket Club">Middlesex County Cricket Club</a> and <a href="/wiki/Marylebone_Cricket_Club" title="Marylebone Cricket Club">Marylebone Cricket Club</a> (MCC). Between 1924 and 1927 he played ten first-class matches, scoring 147 runs at an average of 16.33 with a best score of 37 not out. As a bowler he took 12 wickets at an average of 30.25 with a best of 3 for 43. Three of his first-class games were internationals against <a href="/wiki/Argentine_cricket_team" class="mw-redirect" title="Argentine cricket team">Argentina</a> on the MCC "representative" tour of <a href="/wiki/South_America" title="South America">South America</a> in 1926–27.<sup id="cite_ref-cricinfo_7-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cricinfo-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Dunglass began serving in the <a href="/wiki/Territorial_Army_(United_Kingdom)" class="mw-redirect" title="Territorial Army (United Kingdom)">Territorial Army</a> in 1924 as a <a href="/wiki/Lieutenant_(British_Army)" class="mw-redirect" title="Lieutenant (British Army)">lieutenant</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Lanarkshire_Yeomanry" title="Lanarkshire Yeomanry">Lanarkshire Yeomanry</a>, and was promoted to <a href="/wiki/Captain_(British_Army)" class="mw-redirect" title="Captain (British Army)">captain</a> in 1928.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Professional_career">Professional career</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alec_Douglas-Home&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Professional career">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span id="Member_of_Parliament_.281931.E2.80.931937.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Member_of_Parliament_(1931–1937)">Member of Parliament (1931–1937)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alec_Douglas-Home&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Member of Parliament (1931–1937)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The courtesy title Lord Dunglass did not carry with it membership of the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Lords" title="House of Lords">House of Lords</a>, and Dunglass was eligible to seek election to the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="House of Commons of the United Kingdom">House of Commons</a>. Unlike many aristocratic families, the Douglas-Homes had little history of political service. Uniquely in the family the <a href="/wiki/Cospatrick_Douglas-Home,_11th_Earl_of_Home" title="Cospatrick Douglas-Home, 11th Earl of Home">11th earl</a>, Dunglass's great-grandfather, had held government office, as <a href="/wiki/Under-Secretary_of_State_for_Foreign_Affairs" class="mw-redirect" title="Under-Secretary of State for Foreign Affairs">Under-Secretary</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Foreign_Office" class="mw-redirect" title="Foreign Office">Foreign Office</a> in <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington" title="Arthur Wellesley, 1st Duke of Wellington">Wellington</a>'s <a href="/wiki/First_Wellington_ministry" class="mw-redirect" title="First Wellington ministry">1828–1830 government</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-d2_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-d2-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Dunglass's father stood, reluctantly and unsuccessfully, for Parliament before succeeding to the earldom.<sup id="cite_ref-d2_11-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-d2-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Dunglass had shown little interest in politics while at Eton or Oxford. He had not joined the <a href="/wiki/Oxford_Union" title="Oxford Union">Oxford Union</a> as budding politicians usually did.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> However, as heir to the family estates he was doubtful about the prospect of life as a country gentleman: "I was always rather discontented with this role and felt it wasn't going to be enough."<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> His biographer David Dutton believes that Dunglass became interested in politics because of the widespread unemployment and poverty in the Scottish lowlands where his family lived.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Later in his career, when he had become Prime Minister, Dunglass (by then Sir Alec Douglas-Home) wrote in a memorandum: "I went into politics because I felt that it was a form of public service and that as nearly a generation of politicians had been cut down in the first war those who had anything to give in the way of leadership ought to do so".<sup id="cite_ref-H285_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H285-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;n 1&#93;</a></sup> His political thinking was influenced by that of <a href="/wiki/Noel_Skelton" title="Noel Skelton">Noel Skelton</a>, a member of the <a href="/wiki/Unionist_Party_(Scotland)" title="Unionist Party (Scotland)">Unionist</a> party (as the <a href="/wiki/Conservative_party_(UK)" class="mw-redirect" title="Conservative party (UK)">Conservatives</a> were called in Scotland between 1912 and 1965). Skelton advocated "a property-owning democracy", based on share-options for workers and <a href="/wiki/Industrial_democracy" title="Industrial democracy">industrial democracy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> Dunglass was not persuaded by the socialist ideal of public ownership. He shared Skelton's view that "what everybody owns nobody owns".<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>With Skelton's support Dunglass secured the Unionist candidacy at <a href="/wiki/Coatbridge_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Coatbridge (UK Parliament constituency)">Coatbridge</a> for the 1929 general election.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> It was not a seat that the Unionists expected to win, and he lost to his <a href="/wiki/Labour_Party_(UK)" title="Labour Party (UK)">Labour</a> opponent with 9,210 votes to Labour's 16,879.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> It was, however, valuable experience for Dunglass, who was of a gentle and uncombative disposition and not a natural orator; he began to learn how to deal with hostile audiences and get his message across.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> When a coalition "<a href="/wiki/National_Government_(United_Kingdom)" title="National Government (United Kingdom)">National Government</a>" was formed in 1931 to deal with a financial crisis Dunglass was adopted as the pro-coalition Unionist candidate for <a href="/wiki/Lanark_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Lanark (UK Parliament constituency)">Lanark</a>. The electorate of the area was mixed, and the constituency was not seen as a safe seat for any party; at the 1929 election Labour had captured it from the Unionists. With the backing of the pro-coalition <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_(UK)" title="Liberal Party (UK)">Liberal</a> party, which supported him rather than fielding its own candidate, Dunglass easily beat the Labour candidate.<sup id="cite_ref-d7_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-d7-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Membership of the new House of Commons was overwhelmingly made up of pro-coalition MPs, and there was therefore a large number of eligible members for the government posts to be filled. In Dutton's phrase, "it would have been easy for Dunglass to have languished indefinitely in <a href="/wiki/Backbencher" title="Backbencher">backbench</a> obscurity."<sup id="cite_ref-d7_22-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-d7-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> However, Skelton, appointed as Under-secretary at the <a href="/wiki/Scottish_Office" title="Scottish Office">Scottish Office</a>, offered Dunglass the unpaid post of unofficial parliamentary aide. This was doubly advantageous to Dunglass. Any MP appointed as official <a href="/wiki/Parliamentary_Private_Secretary" title="Parliamentary Private Secretary">Parliamentary Private Secretary</a> (PPS) to a government minister was privy to the inner workings of government but was expected to maintain a discreet silence in the House of Commons. Dunglass achieved the first without having to observe the second.<sup id="cite_ref-d7_22-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-d7-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> He made his maiden speech in February 1932 on the subject of economic policy, advocating a cautiously <a href="/wiki/Protectionism" title="Protectionism">protectionist</a> approach to cheap imports. He countered Labour's objection that this would raise the cost of living, arguing that a tariff "stimulates employment and gives work [and] increases the purchasing power of the people by substituting wages for unemployment benefit."<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During four years as Skelton's aide Dunglass was part of a team working on a wide range of issues, from medical services in rural Scotland to land settlements, fisheries, education, and industry.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> Dunglass was appointed official PPS to Anthony Muirhead, junior minister at the <a href="/wiki/Ministry_of_Labour_(United_Kingdom)" title="Ministry of Labour (United Kingdom)">Ministry of Labour</a>, in 1935, and less than a year later became PPS to the <a href="/wiki/Chancellor_of_the_Exchequer" title="Chancellor of the Exchequer">Chancellor of the Exchequer</a>, <a href="/wiki/Neville_Chamberlain" title="Neville Chamberlain">Neville Chamberlain</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Chamberlain_and_war">Chamberlain and war</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alec_Douglas-Home&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Chamberlain and war">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>By the time of Dunglass's appointment Chamberlain was generally seen as the heir to the premiership,<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> and in 1937 the incumbent, <a href="/wiki/Stanley_Baldwin" title="Stanley Baldwin">Stanley Baldwin</a>, retired, and Chamberlain succeeded him. He retained Dunglass as his PPS, a role described by the biographer <a href="/wiki/D._R._Thorpe" title="D. R. Thorpe">D. R. Thorpe</a> as "the right-hand man&#160;... the eyes and ears of Neville Chamberlain",<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> and by Dutton as "liaison officer with the Parliamentary party, transmitting and receiving information and [keeping] his master informed of the mood on the government's back benches."<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> This was particularly important for Chamberlain, who was often seen as distant and aloof;<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Douglas_Hurd" title="Douglas Hurd">Douglas Hurd</a> wrote that he "lacked the personal charm which makes competent administration palatable to wayward colleagues&#160;– a gift which his parliamentary private secretary possessed in abundance."<sup id="cite_ref-dnb_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dnb-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Dunglass admired Chamberlain, despite his daunting personality: "I liked him, and I think he liked me. But if one went in at the end of the day for a chat or a gossip, he would be inclined to ask 'What do you want?' He was a very difficult man to get to know."<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As Chamberlain's aide Dunglass witnessed at first-hand the Prime Minister's attempts to prevent a second world war through <a href="/wiki/Appeasement" title="Appeasement">appeasement</a> of <a href="/wiki/Adolf_Hitler" title="Adolf Hitler">Adolf Hitler</a>'s Germany. When Chamberlain had his final meeting with Hitler at Munich in September 1938, Dunglass accompanied him. Having gained a short-lived extension of peace by acceding to Hitler's territorial demands at the expense of Czechoslovakia, Chamberlain was welcomed back to London by cheering crowds. Ignoring Dunglass's urging he made an uncharacteristically grandiloquent speech, claiming to have brought back "Peace with Honour" and promising "peace for our time".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHeath1998120Thorpe199785–86_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeath1998120Thorpe199785–86-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> These words were to haunt him when Hitler's continued aggression made war unavoidable less than a year later. Chamberlain remained prime minister from the outbreak of war in September 1939 until May 1940, when, in Dunglass's words, "he could no longer command support of a majority in the Conservative party".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHome197675_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHome197675-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> After a vote in the Commons, in which the government's majority fell from more than 200 to 81, Chamberlain made way for <a href="/wiki/Winston_Churchill" title="Winston Churchill">Winston Churchill</a>. He accepted the non-departmental post of <a href="/wiki/Lord_President_of_the_Council" title="Lord President of the Council">Lord President of the Council</a> in the new coalition government; Dunglass remained as his PPS,<sup id="cite_ref-dnb_30-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dnb-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> having earlier declined the offer of a ministerial post as Under-secretary at the Scottish Office.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThorpe199765_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThorpe199765-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> Although Chamberlain's reputation never recovered from Munich, and his supporters such as <a href="/wiki/Rab_Butler" title="Rab Butler">R. A. Butler</a> suffered throughout their later careers from the "appeasement" tag, Dunglass largely escaped blame.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;n 2&#93;</a></sup> Nevertheless, Dunglass firmly maintained all his life that the Munich agreement had been vital to the survival of Britain and the defeat of Nazi Germany by giving the UK an extra year to prepare for a war that it could not have contested in 1938.<sup id="cite_ref-dnb_30-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dnb-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Within months of his leaving the premiership Chamberlain's health began to fail; he resigned from the cabinet, and died after a short illness in November 1940.
</p><p>Dunglass had volunteered for active military service, seeking to rejoin the Lanarkshire Yeomanry<sup id="cite_ref-dnb_30-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dnb-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> shortly after Chamberlain left Downing Street. The consequent medical examination revealed that Dunglass had a hole in his spine surrounded by <a href="/wiki/Tuberculosis" title="Tuberculosis">tuberculosis</a> in the bone. Without surgery he would have been unable to walk within a matter of months.<sup id="cite_ref-t109_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-t109-38">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> An innovative and hazardous operation was performed in September 1940, lasting six hours, in which the diseased bone in the spine was scraped away and replaced with healthy bone from the patient's shin.<sup id="cite_ref-t109_38-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-t109-38">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1062260506">.mw-parser-output .quotebox{background-color:#F9F9F9;border:1px solid #aaa;box-sizing:border-box;padding:10px;font-size:88%;max-width:100%}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatleft{margin:.5em 1.4em .8em 0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatright{margin:.5em 0 .8em 1.4em}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.centered{overflow:hidden;position:relative;margin:.5em auto .8em auto}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatleft span,.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatright span{font-style:inherit}.mw-parser-output .quotebox>blockquote{margin:0;padding:0;border-left:0;font-family:inherit;font-size:inherit}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-title{background-color:#F9F9F9;text-align:center;font-size:110%;font-weight:bold}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote>:first-child{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote:last-child>:last-child{margin-bottom:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote.quoted:before{font-family:"Times New Roman",serif;font-weight:bold;font-size:large;color:gray;content:" “ ";vertical-align:-45%;line-height:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote.quoted:after{font-family:"Times New Roman",serif;font-weight:bold;font-size:large;color:gray;content:" ” ";line-height:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .left-aligned{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .right-aligned{text-align:right}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .center-aligned{text-align:center}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .quote-title,.mw-parser-output .quotebox .quotebox-quote{display:block}.mw-parser-output .quotebox cite{display:block;font-style:normal}@media screen and (max-width:640px){.mw-parser-output .quotebox{width:100%!important;margin:0 0 .8em!important;float:none!important}}</style><div class="quotebox pullquote floatleft" style="width:200px; ; background-color: #E0E0F8;">
<blockquote class="quotebox-quote left-aligned" style="">
<p><big>You have put backbone into a politician!</big>
</p>
</blockquote>
<p><cite class="right-aligned" style="">Dunglass to his surgeon<sup id="cite_ref-p461_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p461-39">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup></cite>
</p>
</div>
<p>For all of Dunglass's humour and patience, the following two years were a grave trial. He was encased in plaster and kept flat on his back for most of that period. Although buoyed up by the sensitive support of his wife and family, as he later confessed, "I often felt that I would be better dead".<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Towards the end of 1942 he was released from his plaster jacket and fitted with a spinal brace, and in early 1943 he was mobile for the first time since the operation.<sup id="cite_ref-p461_39-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p461-39">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> During his incapacity he read voraciously; among the works he studied were <i><a href="/wiki/Das_Kapital" title="Das Kapital">Das Kapital</a></i>,<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;n 3&#93;</a></sup> and works by <a href="/wiki/Friedrich_Engels" title="Friedrich Engels">Engels</a> and <a href="/wiki/Vladimir_Lenin" title="Vladimir Lenin">Lenin</a>, biographies of nineteenth and twentieth century politicians, and novels by authors from <a href="/wiki/Fyodor_Dostoyevsky" class="mw-redirect" title="Fyodor Dostoyevsky">Dostoyevsky</a> to <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Koestler" title="Arthur Koestler">Koestler</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In July 1943 Dunglass attended the House of Commons for the first time since 1940, and began to make a reputation as a backbench member, particularly for his expertise in the field of foreign affairs.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPike1968461Thorpe1997121_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPike1968461Thorpe1997121-44">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> He foresaw a post-imperial future for Britain and emphasised the need for strong European ties after the war.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997121_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997121-45">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> In 1944, with the war now turning in the Allies' favour, Dunglass spoke eloquently about the importance of resisting the <a href="/wiki/Soviet_Union" title="Soviet Union">Soviet Union</a>'s ambition to dominate eastern Europe. His boldness in publicly urging Churchill not to give in to <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Stalin" title="Joseph Stalin">Joseph Stalin</a> was widely remarked upon; many, including Churchill himself, observed that some of those once associated with appeasement were determined that it should not be repeated in the face of Russian aggression.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997124_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997124-46">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> Labour left the wartime coalition in May 1945 and Churchill formed a caretaker Conservative government, pending a general election in July. Dunglass was appointed to his first ministerial post: <a href="/wiki/Anthony_Eden" title="Anthony Eden">Anthony Eden</a> remained in charge of the Foreign Office, and Dunglass was appointed as one of his two Under-secretaries of State.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDutton200615_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDutton200615-47">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Postwar_and_House_of_Lords_.281945.E2.80.9357.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Postwar_and_House_of_Lords_(1945–57)">Postwar and House of Lords (1945–57)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alec_Douglas-Home&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Postwar and House of Lords (1945–57)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1950, <a href="/wiki/Clement_Attlee" title="Clement Attlee">Clement Attlee</a>, the Labour Prime Minister, called a general election. Dunglass was invited to stand once again as Unionist candidate for Lanark. Having been disgusted at personal attacks during the 1945 campaign by <a href="/wiki/Tom_Steele_(politician)" title="Tom Steele (politician)">Tom Steele</a>, his Labour opponent, Dunglass did not scruple to remind the voters of Lanark that Steele had warmly thanked the Communist Party and its members for helping him take the seat from the Unionists. By 1950, with the <a href="/wiki/Cold_War" title="Cold War">Cold War</a> at its height, Steele's association with the communists was a crucial electoral liability.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> Dunglass regained the seat with one of the smallest majorities in any British constituency: 19,890 to Labour's 19,205.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Labour narrowly won the general election, with a majority of five.<sup id="cite_ref-ts245_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ts245-50">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Hirsel_House,_Coldstream_-_geograph.org.uk_-_93665.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="exterior of large country house" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5f/Hirsel_House%2C_Coldstream_-_geograph.org.uk_-_93665.jpg/220px-Hirsel_House%2C_Coldstream_-_geograph.org.uk_-_93665.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5f/Hirsel_House%2C_Coldstream_-_geograph.org.uk_-_93665.jpg/330px-Hirsel_House%2C_Coldstream_-_geograph.org.uk_-_93665.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5f/Hirsel_House%2C_Coldstream_-_geograph.org.uk_-_93665.jpg/440px-Hirsel_House%2C_Coldstream_-_geograph.org.uk_-_93665.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="425" /></a><figcaption>The Hirsel, the Douglas-Home family's principal residence</figcaption></figure>
<p>In July 1951 the 13th earl died. Dunglass succeeded him, inheriting the title of <a href="/wiki/Earl_of_Home" title="Earl of Home">Earl of Home</a> together with the extensive family estates, including the Hirsel, the Douglas-Homes' principal residence. The new Lord Home took his seat in the Lords; a by-election was called to appoint a new MP for Lanark, but it was still pending when Attlee called another general election in October 1951.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;n 4&#93;</a></sup> The Unionists held Lanark, and the national result gave the Conservatives under Churchill a small but <a href="/wiki/Working_majority" class="mw-redirect" title="Working majority">working</a> majority of seventeen.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Home was appointed to the new post of Minister of State at the Scottish Office, a middle-ranking position, senior to Under-secretary but junior to <a href="/wiki/James_Stuart,_1st_Viscount_Stuart_of_Findhorn" title="James Stuart, 1st Viscount Stuart of Findhorn">James Stuart</a>, the Secretary of State, who was a member of the cabinet. Stuart, previously an influential <a href="/wiki/Chief_whip" class="mw-redirect" title="Chief whip">chief whip</a>, was a confidant of Churchill, and possibly the most powerful Scottish Secretary in any government.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> Thorpe writes that Home owed his appointment to Stuart's advocacy rather than to any great enthusiasm on the Prime Minister's part (Churchill referred to him as "Home sweet Home").<sup id="cite_ref-t141_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-t141-54">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> In addition to his ministerial position Home was appointed to membership of the <a href="/wiki/Privy_Council_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Privy Council of the United Kingdom">Privy Council</a> (PC),<sup id="cite_ref-t141_54-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-t141-54">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> an honour granted only selectively to ministers below cabinet rank.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Royal_Cypher_of_Queen_Elizabeth_II.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Royal cypher consisting of a Crown above the initials E and R with the figure 2 (in Roman numerals) between them" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/Royal_Cypher_of_Queen_Elizabeth_II.svg/100px-Royal_Cypher_of_Queen_Elizabeth_II.svg.png" decoding="async" width="100" height="115" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/Royal_Cypher_of_Queen_Elizabeth_II.svg/150px-Royal_Cypher_of_Queen_Elizabeth_II.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/Royal_Cypher_of_Queen_Elizabeth_II.svg/200px-Royal_Cypher_of_Queen_Elizabeth_II.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="405" data-file-height="465" /></a><figcaption>The royal cypher – a problem for Home and the Scottish Office</figcaption></figure>
<p>Throughout Churchill's second term as Prime Minister (1951–55) Home remained at the Scottish Office, although both Eden at the Foreign Office and <a href="/wiki/Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_5th_Marquess_of_Salisbury" title="Robert Gascoyne-Cecil, 5th Marquess of Salisbury">Lord Salisbury</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Commonwealth_Relations" title="Secretary of State for Commonwealth Relations">Commonwealth Relations Office</a> invited him to join their ministerial teams.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> Among the Scottish matters with which he dealt were hydro-electric projects, hill farming, sea transport, road transport, forestry, and the welfare of <a href="/wiki/Crofter" class="mw-redirect" title="Crofter">crofters</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Scottish_Highlands" title="Scottish Highlands">Highlands</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Outer_Hebrides" title="Outer Hebrides">Western Isles</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-p461_39-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p461-39">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> These matters went largely unreported in the British press, but the question of the <a href="/wiki/Royal_cypher" title="Royal cypher">royal cypher</a> on Post Office pillar boxes became front-page news. Because <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_I_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="Elizabeth I of England">Elizabeth I</a> of England was never queen of Scotland, some nationalists maintained when <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_II" title="Elizabeth II">Elizabeth&#160;II</a> came to the British throne in 1952 that in Scotland she should be styled "Elizabeth I". Churchill said in the House of Commons that considering the "greatness and splendour of Scotland", and the contribution of the Scots to British and world history, "they ought to keep their silliest people in order".<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Home nevertheless arranged that in Scotland new pillar boxes were decorated with the royal crown instead of the full cypher.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>When Eden succeeded Churchill as Prime Minister in 1955 he promoted Home to the cabinet as <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Commonwealth_Relations" title="Secretary of State for Commonwealth Relations">Secretary of State for Commonwealth Relations</a>. At the time of this appointment Home had not been to any of the countries within his ministerial remit, and he quickly arranged to visit Australia, New Zealand, Singapore, India, Pakistan and Ceylon.<sup id="cite_ref-d21_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-d21-60">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> He had to deal with the sensitive subject of immigration from and between Commonwealth countries, where a delicate balance had to be struck between resistance in some quarters in Britain and Australia to non-white immigration on the one hand, and on the other the danger of sanctions in India and Pakistan against British commercial interests if discriminatory policies were pursued.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> In most respects, when Home took up the appointment it seemed to be a relatively uneventful period in the history of the Commonwealth. The upheaval of Indian independence in 1947 was well in the past, and the wave of decolonising of the 1960s was yet to come.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> However, it fell to Home to maintain Commonwealth unity during the <a href="/wiki/Suez_Crisis" title="Suez Crisis">Suez Crisis</a> in 1956, described by Dutton as "the most divisive in its history to date".<sup id="cite_ref-d21_60-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-d21-60">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> Australia, New Zealand and South Africa backed the Anglo-French invasion of Egypt to regain control of the Suez Canal. Canada, Ceylon, India and Pakistan opposed it.<sup id="cite_ref-t178_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-t178-63">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>There appeared to be a real danger that Ceylon, India and, particularly, Pakistan might leave the Commonwealth.<sup id="cite_ref-t178_63-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-t178-63">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Home was firm in his support of the invasion, but used his contacts with <a href="/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru" title="Jawaharlal Nehru">Jawaharlal Nehru</a>, <a href="/wiki/V._K._Krishna_Menon" title="V. K. Krishna Menon">V. K. Krishna Menon</a>, <a href="/wiki/Vijaya_Lakshmi_Pandit" title="Vijaya Lakshmi Pandit">Nan Pandit</a> and others to try to prevent the Commonwealth from breaking up.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> His relationship with Eden was supportive and relaxed; he felt able, as others did not, to warn Eden of unease about Suez both internationally and among some members of the cabinet. Eden dismissed the latter as the "weak sisters";<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> the most prominent was Butler, whose perceived hesitancy over Suez on top of his support for appeasement of Hitler damaged his standing within the Conservative party.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> When the invasion was abandoned under pressure from the US in November 1956, Home worked with the dissenting members of the Commonwealth to build the organisation into what Hurd calls "a modern multiracial Commonwealth".<sup id="cite_ref-dnb_30-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dnb-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Macmillan.27s_government"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Macmillan's_government">Macmillan's government</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alec_Douglas-Home&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Macmillan&#039;s government">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Eden resigned in January 1957. In 1955 he had been the obvious successor to Churchill, but this time there was no clear heir apparent. Leaders of the Conservative party were not elected by ballot of MPs or party members, but emerged after informal soundings within the party, known as "the customary processes of consultation".<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> The chief whip, <a href="/wiki/Edward_Heath" title="Edward Heath">Edward Heath</a>, canvassed the views of backbench Conservative MPs, and two senior Conservative peers, the Lord President of the Council, Lord Salisbury, and the Lord Chancellor, <a href="/wiki/David_Maxwell_Fyfe,_1st_Earl_of_Kilmuir" title="David Maxwell Fyfe, 1st Earl of Kilmuir">Lord Kilmuir</a>, saw members of the cabinet individually to ascertain their preferences. Only one cabinet colleague supported Butler; the rest, including Home, opted for Macmillan. Churchill, whom the Queen consulted, did the same.<sup id="cite_ref-t189_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-t189-68">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Macmillan was appointed Prime Minister on 10 January 1957.<sup id="cite_ref-t189_68-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-t189-68">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the new administration Home remained at the Commonwealth Relations Office.  Much of his time was spent on matters relating to Africa, where the futures of <a href="/wiki/Bechuanaland" class="mw-redirect" title="Bechuanaland">Bechuanaland</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Central_African_Federation" class="mw-redirect" title="Central African Federation">Central African Federation</a> needed to be agreed. Among other matters in which he was involved were the dispute between India and Pakistan over <a href="/wiki/Kashmir" title="Kashmir">Kashmir</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ten_Pound_Poms" title="Ten Pound Poms">assisted emigration</a> from Britain to Australia, and relations with <a href="/wiki/Makarios_III" title="Makarios III">Archbishop Makarios</a> of Cyprus. The last unexpectedly led to an enhanced cabinet role for Home. Makarios, leader of the militant anti-British and pro-Greek movement, was detained in exile in the <a href="/wiki/Seychelles" title="Seychelles">Seychelles</a>. Macmillan, with the agreement of Home and most of the cabinet, decided that this imprisonment was doing more harm than good to Britain's position in Cyprus, and ordered Makarios's release. Lord Salisbury strongly dissented from the decision and resigned from the cabinet in March 1957. Macmillan added Salisbury's responsibilities to Home's existing duties, making him Lord President of the Council and <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_House_of_Lords" title="Leader of the House of Lords">Leader of the House of Lords</a>. The first of these posts was largely honorific, but the leadership of the Lords put Home in charge of getting the government's business through the upper house, and brought him nearer to the centre of power.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> In Hurd's phrase, "By the imperceptible process characteristic of British politics he found himself month by month, without any particular manoeuvre on his part, becoming an indispensable figure in the government."<sup id="cite_ref-dnb_30-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dnb-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Iain_Macleod_crop.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="middle-aged man, bald, speaking into a microphone" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Iain_Macleod_crop.jpg/170px-Iain_Macleod_crop.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="197" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Iain_Macleod_crop.jpg/255px-Iain_Macleod_crop.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/2/22/Iain_Macleod_crop.jpg 2x" data-file-width="314" data-file-height="364" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Iain_Macleod" title="Iain Macleod">Iain Macleod</a>, who had a difficult relationship with Home</figcaption></figure>
<p>Home was generally warmly regarded by colleagues and opponents alike, and there were few politicians who did not respond well to him. One was Attlee, but as their political primes did not overlap this was of minor consequence.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> More important was <a href="/wiki/Iain_Macleod" title="Iain Macleod">Iain Macleod</a>'s prickly relationship with Home. Macleod, <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_the_Colonies" title="Secretary of State for the Colonies">Secretary of State for the Colonies</a> from 1959 to 1961, was, like Butler, on the liberal wing of the Conservative party; he was convinced, as Home was not, that Britain's colonies in Africa should have majority rule and independence as quickly as possible. Their spheres of influence overlapped in the Central African Federation.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;n 5&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Macleod wished to push ahead with majority rule and independence; Home believed in a more gradual approach to independence, accommodating both white minority and black majority opinions and interests. Macleod disagreed with those who warned that precipitate independence would lead the newly independent nations into "trouble, strife, poverty, dictatorship" and other evils.<sup id="cite_ref-frankel_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-frankel-73">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> His reply was, "Would you want the Romans to have stayed on in Britain?"<sup id="cite_ref-frankel_73-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-frankel-73">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> He threatened to resign unless he was allowed to release the leading <a href="/wiki/Nyasaland" title="Nyasaland">Nyasaland</a> activist <a href="/wiki/Hastings_Banda" title="Hastings Banda">Hastings Banda</a> from prison, a move that Home and others thought unwise and liable to provoke distrust of Britain among the white minority in the federation.<sup id="cite_ref-macleod_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-macleod-74">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> Macleod had his way, but by that time Home was no longer at the Commonwealth Relations Office.<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Foreign_Secretary_.281960.E2.80.931963.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_Secretary_(1960–1963)">Foreign Secretary (1960–1963)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alec_Douglas-Home&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Foreign Secretary (1960–1963)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1960 the Chancellor of the Exchequer, <a href="/wiki/Derick_Heathcoat-Amory" class="mw-redirect" title="Derick Heathcoat-Amory">Derick Heathcoat-Amory</a>, insisted on retiring.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> Macmillan agreed with Heathcoat-Amory that the best successor at the <a href="/wiki/HM_Treasury" title="HM Treasury">Treasury</a> would be the current Foreign Secretary, <a href="/wiki/Selwyn_Lloyd" title="Selwyn Lloyd">Selwyn Lloyd</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>  In terms of ability and experience the obvious candidate to take over from Lloyd at the Foreign Office was Home,<sup id="cite_ref-dnb_30-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dnb-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> but by 1960 there was an expectation that the Foreign Secretary would be a member of the House of Commons. The post had not been held by a peer since <a href="/wiki/E._F._L._Wood,_1st_Earl_of_Halifax" class="mw-redirect" title="E. F. L. Wood, 1st Earl of Halifax">Lord Halifax</a> in 1938–40; Eden had wished to appoint Salisbury in 1955, but concluded that it would be unacceptable to the Commons.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Sir_Edward_Heath.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1f/Sir_Edward_Heath.jpg/170px-Sir_Edward_Heath.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="212" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1f/Sir_Edward_Heath.jpg/255px-Sir_Edward_Heath.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1f/Sir_Edward_Heath.jpg/340px-Sir_Edward_Heath.jpg 2x" data-file-width="581" data-file-height="724" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Edward_Heath" title="Edward Heath">Edward Heath</a>, Home's deputy at the Foreign Office. They later served in each other's cabinets.</figcaption></figure>
<p>After discussions with Lloyd and senior civil servants, Macmillan took the unprecedented step of appointing two Foreign Office cabinet ministers: Home, as Foreign Secretary, in the Lords, and Edward Heath, as <a href="/wiki/Lord_Privy_Seal" title="Lord Privy Seal">Lord Privy Seal</a> and deputy Foreign Secretary, in the Commons. With British application for admission to the <a href="/wiki/European_Economic_Community" title="European Economic Community">European Economic Community</a> (EEC) pending, Heath was given particular responsibility for the EEC negotiations as well as for speaking in the Commons on foreign affairs in general.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The opposition Labour party protested at Home's appointment; its leader, <a href="/wiki/Hugh_Gaitskell" title="Hugh Gaitskell">Hugh Gaitskell</a>, said that it was "constitutionally objectionable" for a peer to be in charge of the Foreign Office.<sup id="cite_ref-pike462_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pike462-80">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> Macmillan responded that an accident of birth should not be allowed to deny him the services of "the best man for the job&#160;– the man I want at my side".<sup id="cite_ref-pike462_80-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pike462-80">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> Hurd comments, "Like all such artificial commotions it died down after a time (and indeed was not renewed with any strength nineteen years later when <a href="/wiki/Margaret_Thatcher" title="Margaret Thatcher">Margaret Thatcher</a> appointed another peer, <a href="/wiki/Peter_Carington,_6th_Baron_Carrington" title="Peter Carington, 6th Baron Carrington">Lord Carrington</a>, to the same post)".<sup id="cite_ref-dnb_30-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dnb-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> The Home–Heath partnership worked well. Despite their different backgrounds and ages&#160;– Home an <a href="/wiki/Edwardian_era" title="Edwardian era">Edwardian</a> aristocrat and Heath a lower-middle class meritocrat raised in the inter-war years&#160;– the two men respected and liked one another. Home supported Macmillan's ambition to get Britain into the EEC, and was happy to leave the negotiations in Heath's hands.<sup id="cite_ref-dnb_30-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dnb-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Home's attention was mainly concentrated on the Cold War, where his forcefully expressed anti-communist beliefs were tempered by a pragmatic approach to dealing with the Soviet Union. His first major problem in this sphere was in 1961 when on the orders of the Soviet leader, <a href="/wiki/Nikita_Khrushchev" title="Nikita Khrushchev">Nikita Khrushchev</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Berlin_Wall" title="Berlin Wall">Berlin Wall</a> was erected to stop <a href="/wiki/East_Germany" title="East Germany">East Germans</a> escaping to <a href="/wiki/West_Germany" title="West Germany">West Germany</a> via <a href="/wiki/West_Berlin" title="West Berlin">West Berlin</a>. Home wrote to his American counterpart, <a href="/wiki/Dean_Rusk" title="Dean Rusk">Dean Rusk</a>, "The prevention of East Berliners getting into West Berlin has never been a <i>casus belli</i> for us. We are concerned with Western access to Berlin and that is what we must maintain."<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> The governments of West Germany, Britain and the US quickly reached agreement on their joint negotiating position; it remained to persuade <a href="/wiki/Charles_de_Gaulle" title="Charles de Gaulle">President de Gaulle</a> of France to align himself with the allies. During their discussions Macmillan commented that de Gaulle showed "all the rigidity of a poker without its occasional warmth."<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> An agreement was reached, and the allies tacitly recognised that the wall was going to remain in place. The Soviets for their part did not seek to cut off allied access to West Berlin through East German territory.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The following year the <a href="/wiki/Cuban_Missile_Crisis" title="Cuban Missile Crisis">Cuban Missile Crisis</a> threatened to turn the Cold War into a nuclear one. Soviet nuclear missiles were brought to Cuba, provocatively close to the US. The American president, <a href="/wiki/John_F._Kennedy" title="John F. Kennedy">John F Kennedy</a>, insisted that they must be removed, and many thought that the world was on the brink of catastrophe with nuclear exchanges between the two super-powers.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>  Despite a public image of unflappable calm, Macmillan was by nature nervous and highly strung.<sup id="cite_ref-dnb_30-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dnb-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> During the missile crisis, Home, whose calm was genuine and innate, strengthened the Prime Minister's resolve, and encouraged him to back up Kennedy's defiance of Soviet threats of nuclear attack.<sup id="cite_ref-dnb_30-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dnb-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>  The Lord Chancellor (<a href="/wiki/Reginald_Manningham-Buller,_1st_Viscount_Dilhorne" title="Reginald Manningham-Buller, 1st Viscount Dilhorne">Lord Dilhorne</a>), the <a href="/wiki/Attorney_General_for_England_and_Wales" title="Attorney General for England and Wales">Attorney General</a> (<a href="/wiki/John_Hobson_(politician)" title="John Hobson (politician)">Sir John Hobson</a>) and the <a href="/wiki/Solicitor_General_for_England_and_Wales" title="Solicitor General for England and Wales">Solicitor General</a>, (<a href="/wiki/Peter_Rawlinson,_Baron_Rawlinson_of_Ewell" title="Peter Rawlinson, Baron Rawlinson of Ewell">Sir Peter Rawlinson</a>) privately gave Home their opinion that the American blockade of Cuba was a breach of international law,<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;n 6&#93;</a></sup> but he continued to advocate a policy of strong support for Kennedy.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> When Khrushchev backed down and removed the Soviet missiles from Cuba, Home commented:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>There has been a good deal of speculation about Russia's motives. To me they are quite clear. Their motive was to test the will of the United States and to see how the President of the United States, in particular, would react against a threat of force. If the President had failed for one moment in a matter which affected the security of the United States, no ally of America would have had confidence in United States protection ever again.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>The principal landmark of Home's term as Foreign Secretary was also in the sphere of east–west relations: the negotiation and signature of the <a href="/wiki/Partial_Nuclear_Test_Ban_Treaty" title="Partial Nuclear Test Ban Treaty">Partial Nuclear Test Ban Treaty</a> in 1963. He got on well with his American and Soviet counterparts, Rusk and <a href="/wiki/Andrei_Gromyko" title="Andrei Gromyko">Andrei Gromyko</a>. The latter wrote that whenever he met Home there were "no sudden, still less brilliant, breakthroughs" but "each meeting left a civilised impression that made the next meeting easier." Gromyko concluded that Home added sharpness to British foreign policy.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> Gromyko, Home and Rusk signed the treaty in Moscow on 5 August 1963.<sup id="cite_ref-timesban_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-timesban-91">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> After the fear provoked internationally by the Cuban Missile Crisis, the ban on nuclear testing in the atmosphere, in outer space and under water was widely welcomed as a step towards ending the cold war.<sup id="cite_ref-timesban_91-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-timesban-91">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> For the British government the good news from Moscow was doubly welcome for drawing attention away from the <a href="/wiki/Profumo_affair" title="Profumo affair">Profumo affair</a>, a sexual scandal involving <a href="/wiki/John_Profumo" title="John Profumo">a senior minister</a>, which had left Macmillan's administration looking vulnerable.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Successor_to_Macmillan">Successor to Macmillan</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alec_Douglas-Home&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Successor to Macmillan">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Quintin_Hogg,_Baron_Hailsham_Allan_Warren.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="An elderly man, clean shaven, with full head of grey hair" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Quintin_Hogg%2C_Baron_Hailsham_Allan_Warren.jpg/170px-Quintin_Hogg%2C_Baron_Hailsham_Allan_Warren.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="236" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Quintin_Hogg%2C_Baron_Hailsham_Allan_Warren.jpg/255px-Quintin_Hogg%2C_Baron_Hailsham_Allan_Warren.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Quintin_Hogg%2C_Baron_Hailsham_Allan_Warren.jpg/340px-Quintin_Hogg%2C_Baron_Hailsham_Allan_Warren.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1838" data-file-height="2553" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Quintin_Hogg,_Baron_Hailsham_of_St_Marylebone" title="Quintin Hogg, Baron Hailsham of St Marylebone">Lord Hailsham</a>, Macmillan's original preference as successor</figcaption></figure>
<p>In October 1963, just before the Conservative party's annual conference, Macmillan was taken ill with a <a href="/wiki/Prostate" title="Prostate">prostatic obstruction</a>. The condition was at first thought more serious than it turned out to be, and he announced that he would resign as Prime Minister as soon as a successor was appointed. Three senior politicians were considered likely successors, Butler (<a href="/wiki/First_Secretary_of_State" title="First Secretary of State">First Secretary of State</a>), <a href="/wiki/Reginald_Maudling" title="Reginald Maudling">Reginald Maudling</a> (Chancellor of the Exchequer) and <a href="/wiki/Quintin_Hogg,_Baron_Hailsham_of_St_Marylebone" title="Quintin Hogg, Baron Hailsham of St Marylebone">Lord Hailsham</a> (Lord President of the Council and Leader of the House of Lords). <i><a href="/wiki/The_Times" title="The Times">The Times</a></i> summed up their support:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Mr. Butler can no doubt be sure of a majority inside the Cabinet, where the main initiative must now be taken. Mr. Maudling, when Parliament dispersed at the beginning of August, could have commanded a majority among backbenchers in the Commons. Lord Hailsham, as his reception showed today on his first appearance before the conference, continues to be the darling of the constituency associations.<sup id="cite_ref-t63_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-t63-93">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>In the same article, Home was mentioned in passing as "a fourth hypothetical candidate" on whom the party could compromise if necessary.<sup id="cite_ref-t63_93-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-t63-93">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>It was assumed in the <i>Times</i> article, and by other commentators, that if Hailsham (or Home) was a candidate he would have to renounce his peerage.<sup id="cite_ref-t63_93-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-t63-93">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> This had been made possible for the first time by recent legislation.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;n 7&#93;</a></sup> The last British Prime Minister to sit in the House of Lords was the third <a href="/wiki/Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury" title="Robert Gascoyne-Cecil, 3rd Marquess of Salisbury">Marquess of Salisbury</a> in 1902. By 1923, having to choose between Baldwin and <a href="/wiki/George_Curzon,_1st_Marquess_Curzon_of_Kedleston" title="George Curzon, 1st Marquess Curzon of Kedleston">Lord Curzon</a>, <a href="/wiki/George_V" title="George V">George V</a> decided that "the requirements of the present times" obliged him to appoint a Prime Minister from the Commons. His private secretary recorded that the King "believed he would not be fulfilling his trust were he now to make his selection of Prime Minister from the House of Lords".<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> Similarly, after the resignation of Neville Chamberlain in 1940 there were two likely successors, Churchill and Halifax, but the latter ruled himself out for the premiership on the grounds that his membership of the House of Lords disqualified him.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> In 1963, therefore, it was well established that the Prime Minister should be a member of the House of Commons.<sup id="cite_ref-timesobit_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-timesobit-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> On 10 October Hailsham announced his intention to renounce his viscountcy.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
The "customary processes" once again took place. The usual privacy of the consultations was made impossible because they took place during the party conference, and the potential successors made their bids very publicly. Butler had the advantage of giving the party leader's keynote address to the conference in Macmillan's absence, but was widely thought to have wasted the opportunity by delivering an uninspiring speech.<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> Hailsham put off many potential backers by his extrovert, and some thought vulgar, campaigning.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> Maudling, like Butler, made a speech that failed to impress the conference. Senior Conservative figures such as <a href="/wiki/Frederick_Marquis,_1st_Earl_of_Woolton" title="Frederick Marquis, 1st Earl of Woolton">Lord Woolton</a> and Selwyn Lloyd urged Home to make himself available for consideration.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Having ruled himself out of the race when the news of  Macmillan's illness broke, Home angered at least two of his cabinet colleagues by changing his mind.<sup id="cite_ref-timesobit_2-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-timesobit-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> Macmillan quickly came to the view that Home would be the best choice as his successor, and gave him valuable behind-the-scenes backing. He let it be known that if he recovered he would be willing to serve as a member of a Home cabinet.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> He had earlier favoured Hailsham, but changed his mind when he learned from <a href="/wiki/David_Ormsby-Gore,_5th_Baron_Harlech" title="David Ormsby-Gore, 5th Baron Harlech">Lord Harlech</a>, the British ambassador to the US, that the Kennedy administration was uneasy at the prospect of Hailsham as Prime Minister,<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> and from his chief whip that Hailsham, seen as a right-winger, would alienate moderate voters.<sup id="cite_ref-t303_105-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-t303-105">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Butler, by contrast, was seen as on the liberal wing of the Conservatives, and his election as leader might split the party.<sup id="cite_ref-t303_105-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-t303-105">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> The Lord Chancellor, Lord Dilhorne, conducted a poll of cabinet members, and reported to Macmillan that taking account of first and second preferences there were ten votes for Home, four for Maudling, three for Butler and two for Hailsham.<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;n 8&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The appointment of a Prime Minister remained part of the <a href="/wiki/Royal_prerogative" title="Royal prerogative">royal prerogative</a>, on which the monarch had no constitutional duty to consult an outgoing Prime Minister. Nevertheless, Macmillan advised the Queen that he considered Home the right choice.<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> Little of this was known beyond the senior ranks of the party and the royal secretariat. On 18 October <i>The Times</i> ran the headline, "The Queen May Send for Mr. Butler Today".<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> <i><a href="/wiki/The_Daily_Telegraph" title="The Daily Telegraph">The Daily Telegraph</a></i> and <i><a href="/wiki/The_Financial_Times" class="mw-redirect" title="The Financial Times">The Financial Times</a></i> also predicted that Butler was about to be appointed.<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> The Queen sent for Home the same day. Aware of the divisions within the governing party, she did not appoint him Prime Minister, but invited him to see whether he was able to form a government.<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
Home's cabinet colleagues <a href="/wiki/Enoch_Powell" title="Enoch Powell">Enoch Powell</a> and Iain Macleod, who disapproved of his candidacy, made a last-minute effort to prevent him from taking office by trying to persuade Butler and the other candidates not to take posts in a Home cabinet.<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> Butler, however, believed it to be his duty to serve in the cabinet;<sup id="cite_ref-dnb_30-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dnb-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> he refused to have any part in the conspiracy, and accepted the post of Foreign Secretary.<sup id="cite_ref-h321_114-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-h321-114">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> The other candidates followed Butler's lead and only Powell and Macleod held out and refused office under Home.<sup id="cite_ref-h321_114-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-h321-114">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> Macleod commented, "One does not expect to have many people with one in the last ditch".<sup id="cite_ref-macleod_74-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-macleod-74">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>  On 19 October Home was able to return to <a href="/wiki/Buckingham_Palace" title="Buckingham Palace">Buckingham Palace</a> to <a href="/wiki/Kissing_hands" title="Kissing hands">kiss hands</a> as Prime Minister.<sup id="cite_ref-p463_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p463-115">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> The press was not only wrong-footed by the appointment, but generally highly critical. The pro-Labour <i><a href="/wiki/Daily_Mirror" title="Daily Mirror">Daily Mirror</a></i> said on its front page:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>A nice chap and a polite peer. But <a href="/wiki/Caligula" title="Caligula">Caligula</a>'s appointment of <a href="/wiki/Incitatus" title="Incitatus">his horse</a> as a consul was an act of prudent statesmanship compared with this gesture of sickbed levity by Mr. Macmillan.&#160;... Alec (not Smart Alec&#160;– just Alec) is playing chess with a Cabinet containing at least four members of greater stature, brain-power, personality and potential than himself. Butler has been betrayed, Maudling insulted, Macleod ignored, Heath treated with contempt, and Hailsham giggled out of court by the jester in hospital.<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p><i>The Times</i>, generally pro-Conservative, had backed Butler,<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> and called it "prodigal" of the party to pass over his many talents. The paper praised Home as "an outstandingly successful Foreign Secretary", but doubted his grasp of domestic affairs, his modernising instincts and his suitability "to carry the Conservative Party through a fierce and probably dirty campaign" at the general election due within a year.<sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> <i><a href="/wiki/The_Guardian" title="The Guardian">The Guardian</a></i>, liberal in its political outlook, remarked that Home "does not look like the man to impart force and purpose to his Cabinet and the country" and suggested that he seemed too frail politically to be even a stop-gap.<sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> <i><a href="/wiki/The_Observer" title="The Observer">The Observer</a></i>, another liberal-minded paper, said, "The overwhelming&#160;– and damaging&#160;– impression left by the events of the last two weeks is that the Tories have been forced to settle for a second-best.&#160;... The calmness and steadiness  which made him a good Foreign Secretary, particularly at times of crisis like Berlin and Cuba, may also be a liability."<sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In January 1964, and in the absence of any other information, Macleod now editor of <i><a href="/wiki/The_Spectator_(1828)" class="mw-redirect" title="The Spectator (1828)">The Spectator</a></i>, used the pretext of a <a href="/wiki/The_Spectator_(1828)#&quot;The_Tory_Leadership&quot;_article" class="mw-redirect" title="The Spectator (1828)">review of a book</a> by <a href="/wiki/Randolph_Churchill" title="Randolph Churchill">Randolph Churchill</a> to publicise his own different and very detailed version of the leadership election.  He described the "soundings" of five Tory grandees, four of whom, like Home and Macmillan had been to school at Eton, as a stitch up by an Etonian 'magic circle.'<sup id="cite_ref-vernon_121-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-vernon-121">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup>  The article received wide publicity convincing <a href="/wiki/Anthony_Howard_(journalist)" title="Anthony Howard (journalist)">Anthony Howard</a>, who later declared himself  "deeply affronted ...and never more affronted than when Alec Douglas-Home became leader of the Conservative Party."<sup id="cite_ref-ciar_122-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ciar-122">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Prime_minister_.281963.E2.80.931964.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_minister_(1963–1964)">Prime minister (1963–1964)<span class="anchor" id="Premiership"></span></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alec_Douglas-Home&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Prime minister (1963–1964)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Douglas-Home_ministry" class="mw-redirect" title="Douglas-Home ministry">Douglas-Home ministry</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"><table class="infobox" style="padding-bottom:2px; padding-top:2px;"><tbody><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-image" style="border-bottom:0; ;"><span class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Frameless"><a href="/wiki/File:Alec_Douglas-Home_in_1963.jpg" class="mw-file-description" title="Alec Douglas-Home"><img alt="Alec Douglas-Home" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/Alec_Douglas-Home_in_1963.jpg/220px-Alec_Douglas-Home_in_1963.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/Alec_Douglas-Home_in_1963.jpg/330px-Alec_Douglas-Home_in_1963.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/Alec_Douglas-Home_in_1963.jpg/440px-Alec_Douglas-Home_in_1963.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1396" data-file-height="1861" /></a></span><div class="infobox-caption" style="line-height:normal;
