padding-top:2px;">Douglas-Home in 1963</div></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-full-data" style="padding:0;"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-full-data" style="border-bottom:none"><span class="nowrap"><b><span style="font-size:120%">Premiership of Alec Douglas-Home</span></b></span><br />19 October 1963&#160;–&#32;16 October 1964</td><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label" style="text-align:left">Monarch</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_II" title="Elizabeth II">Elizabeth II</a></td><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label" style="text-align:left">Cabinet</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Douglas-Home_ministry" class="mw-redirect" title="Douglas-Home ministry">Douglas-Home ministry</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label" style="text-align:left">Party</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK)" title="Conservative Party (UK)">Conservative</a></td><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label" style="text-align:left"><a href="/wiki/Seat_of_government" title="Seat of government">Seat</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/10_Downing_Street" title="10 Downing Street">10 Downing Street</a></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-full-data"><div style="line-height:normal; padding-top:1px;"><div style="width:100%"><div style="float: left; text-align:left;padding-right:0.5em;" class="noprint">&#8592;&#160;<a href="/wiki/Premiership_of_Harold_Macmillan" class="mw-redirect" title="Premiership of Harold Macmillan">Harold Macmillan</a></div><div style="float: right; text-align:right;padding-left:0.5em;" class="noprint"><a href="/wiki/First_premiership_of_Harold_Wilson" class="mw-redirect" title="First premiership of Harold Wilson">Harold Wilson</a>&#160;&#8594;</div></div></div></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">
                                                   <hr class="nomobile" style="background:#eee; height:5px; clear:both; margin:0 0 8px;" /> <div class="center"><div style="display:inline-block; padding:6px 0 2px;"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Royal_Coat_of_Arms_of_the_United_Kingdom_(HM_Government,_1952-2022).svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/Royal_Coat_of_Arms_of_the_United_Kingdom_%28HM_Government%2C_1952-2022%29.svg/100px-Royal_Coat_of_Arms_of_the_United_Kingdom_%28HM_Government%2C_1952-2022%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="100" height="82" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/Royal_Coat_of_Arms_of_the_United_Kingdom_%28HM_Government%2C_1952-2022%29.svg/150px-Royal_Coat_of_Arms_of_the_United_Kingdom_%28HM_Government%2C_1952-2022%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/Royal_Coat_of_Arms_of_the_United_Kingdom_%28HM_Government%2C_1952-2022%29.svg/200px-Royal_Coat_of_Arms_of_the_United_Kingdom_%28HM_Government%2C_1952-2022%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1530" data-file-height="1256" /></a></span></div><br /><div style="line-height:normal; min-height:1px; padding-bottom:2px;"><a href="/wiki/Coat_of_arms_of_HM_Government" class="mw-redirect" title="Coat of arms of HM Government">Coat of arms of HM Government</a></div></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>On 23 October 1963, four days after becoming Prime Minister, Home disclaimed his earldom and associated lesser peerages.<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;n 9&#93;</a></sup> Having been made a <a href="/wiki/Knight_of_the_Order_of_the_Thistle" class="mw-redirect" title="Knight of the Order of the Thistle">Knight of the Order of the Thistle</a> (KT) in 1962, he was known after stepping down from the Lords as Sir Alec Douglas-Home.<sup id="cite_ref-dnb_30-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dnb-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> The safe Unionist seat of <a href="/wiki/Kinross_and_Western_Perthshire_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Kinross and Western Perthshire (UK Parliament constituency)">Kinross and West Perthshire</a> was vacant, and Douglas-Home was adopted as his party's candidate. Parliament was due to meet on 24 October after the summer recess, but its return was postponed until 12 November pending the by-election.<sup id="cite_ref-p464_125-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p464-125">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> For twenty days Douglas-Home was Prime Minister while a member of neither house of Parliament, a situation without modern precedent.<sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;n 10&#93;</a></sup> He won the by-election with a majority of 9,328; the Liberal candidate was in second place and Labour in third.<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Parliamentary leader of the opposition Labour party, <a href="/wiki/Harold_Wilson" title="Harold Wilson">Harold Wilson</a>, attacked the new prime minister as "an elegant anachronism". He asserted that nobody from Douglas-Home's background knew of the problems of ordinary families. In particular, Wilson demanded to know how "a scion of an effete establishment" could lead the technological revolution that Wilson held to be necessary: "This is the counter-revolution&#160;... After half a century of democratic advance, of social revolution, the whole process has ground to a halt with a fourteenth earl!"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPike1968463_130-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPike1968463-130">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> Douglas-Home dismissed this as <a href="/wiki/Snob#inverted_snobbery" title="Snob">inverted snobbery</a>, and observed, "I suppose Mr Wilson, when you come to think of it, is the fourteenth Mr Wilson."<sup id="cite_ref-dnb_30-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dnb-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPimlott19923_131-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPimlott19923-131">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> He called Wilson "this slick salesman of synthetic science" and the Labour party "the only relic of class consciousness in the country".<sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> The opposition retreated, with a statement in the press that "The Labour Party is not interested in the fact that the new prime minister inherited a fourteenth Earldom&#160;– he cannot help his antecedents any more than the rest of us."<sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Douglas-Home inherited from Macmillan a government widely perceived as in decline; Hurd wrote that it was "becalmed in a sea of satire and scandal."<sup id="cite_ref-dnb_30-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dnb-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Douglas-Home was the target of satirists on <a href="/wiki/BBC_television" class="mw-redirect" title="BBC television">BBC television</a> and in <i><a href="/wiki/Private_Eye" title="Private Eye">Private Eye</a></i> magazine. <i>Private Eye</i> persistently referred to him as "Baillie Vass", in allusion to a Scottish <a href="/wiki/Bailie" title="Bailie">bailie</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;n 11&#93;</a></sup> Unlike Wilson, Douglas-Home was not at ease on television, and came across as less spontaneous than his opponent.<sup id="cite_ref-timesobit_2-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-timesobit-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:JFKWHP-KN-C30678_(cropped).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Three figures standing and conversing. Jacqueline Kennedy wears a navy dress and has her back to the camera; she is flanked by Douglas-Home (left) and Edward Kennedy (right), both in morning dress." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c0/JFKWHP-KN-C30678_%28cropped%29.jpg/220px-JFKWHP-KN-C30678_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="236" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c0/JFKWHP-KN-C30678_%28cropped%29.jpg/330px-JFKWHP-KN-C30678_%28cropped%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/c/c0/JFKWHP-KN-C30678_%28cropped%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="367" data-file-height="393" /></a><figcaption>Douglas-Home with <a href="/wiki/Jacqueline_Kennedy_Onassis" title="Jacqueline Kennedy Onassis">Jackie</a> (centre, back to camera) and <a href="/wiki/Ted_Kennedy" title="Ted Kennedy">Ted Kennedy</a> (right) at the <a href="/wiki/State_funeral_of_John_F._Kennedy" title="State funeral of John F. Kennedy">post funeral reception</a> for John F. Kennedy, 25 November 1963</figcaption></figure>
<p>In international affairs, the most dramatic event during Douglas-Home's premiership was the <a href="/wiki/Assassination_of_John_F._Kennedy" title="Assassination of John F. Kennedy">assassination of John F. Kennedy</a>, which happened about a month after the start of his tenure. Douglas-Home broadcast a tribute on television.<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> He had liked and worked well with Kennedy, and did not develop such a satisfactory relationship with <a href="/wiki/Lyndon_Johnson" class="mw-redirect" title="Lyndon Johnson">Lyndon Johnson</a>. Their governments had a serious disagreement on the question of British trade with Cuba.<sup id="cite_ref-dnb_30-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dnb-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Under Douglas-Home the colonies of <a href="/wiki/Northern_Rhodesia" title="Northern Rhodesia">Northern Rhodesia</a> and Nyasaland gained independence, though this was as a result of negotiations led by Macleod under the Macmillan government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewsom2001114_139-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewsom2001114-139">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In Britain there was economic prosperity; exports "zoomed", according to <i>The Times</i>, and the economy was growing at an annual rate of four per cent.<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> Douglas-Home made no pretence to economic expertise; he commented that his problems were of two sorts: "The political ones are insoluble and the economic ones are incomprehensible."<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> On another occasion he said, "When I have to read economic documents I have to have a box of matches and start moving them into position to simplify and illustrate the points to myself."<sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup>  He left Maudling in charge at the Treasury, and promoted Heath to a new business and economic portfolio. The latter took the lead in the one substantial piece of domestic legislation of Douglas-Home's premiership, the abolition of <a href="/wiki/Resale_price_maintenance" title="Resale price maintenance">resale price maintenance</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-dnb_30-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dnb-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Resale_Prices_Act_1964" class="mw-redirect" title="Resale Prices Act 1964">Resale Prices Bill</a> was introduced to deny manufacturers and suppliers the power to stipulate the prices at which their goods must be sold by the retailer. At the time, up to forty per cent of goods sold in Britain were subject to such price fixing, to the detriment of competition and to the disadvantage of the consumer.<sup id="cite_ref-rpm_143-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-rpm-143">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> Douglas-Home, less instinctively liberal on economic matters than Heath, would probably not have sponsored such a proposal unprompted,<sup id="cite_ref-dnb_30-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dnb-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> but he gave Heath his backing, in the face of opposition from some cabinet colleagues, including Butler, Hailsham and Lloyd,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERoth1972176_144-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERoth1972176-144">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> and a substantial number of Conservative backbenchers. They believed the change would benefit supermarkets and other large retailers at the expense of proprietors of small shops.<sup id="cite_ref-rpm_143-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-rpm-143">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> The government was forced to make concessions to avoid defeat. Retail price maintenance would continue to be legal for some goods; these included books, on which it remained in force until market forces led to its abandonment in 1995.<sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> Manufacturers and suppliers would also be permitted to refuse to supply any retailer who sold their goods at less than cost price, as a <a href="/wiki/Loss_leader" title="Loss leader">loss leader</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-rpm_143-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-rpm-143">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> The bill had a difficult Parliamentary passage during which the Labour party generally abstained, leaving the Conservatives to vote for or against their own government. The bill received the <a href="/wiki/Royal_assent" title="Royal assent">royal assent</a> in July 1964, but did not become operative until 1965, by which time Douglas-Home, Heath and their colleagues were out of office.<sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A plot to kidnap Douglas-Home in April 1964 was foiled by the Prime Minister himself. Two left-wing students from the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Aberdeen" title="University of Aberdeen">University of Aberdeen</a> followed him to the house of <a href="/wiki/John_Buchan,_2nd_Baron_Tweedsmuir" title="John Buchan, 2nd Baron Tweedsmuir">John</a> and <a href="/wiki/Priscilla_Buchan,_Baroness_Tweedsmuir_of_Belhelvie" title="Priscilla Buchan, Baroness Tweedsmuir of Belhelvie">Priscilla Buchan</a>, where he was staying. He was alone at the time and answered the door, where the students told him that they planned to kidnap him. He responded, "I suppose you realise if you do, the Conservatives will win the election by 200 or 300." He gave his intending abductors some beer, and they abandoned their plot.<sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;n 12&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Harold_Wilson_(1967).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Middle-aged man, clean shaven, full head of greying hair" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/Harold_Wilson_%281967%29.jpg/170px-Harold_Wilson_%281967%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="227" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/Harold_Wilson_%281967%29.jpg/255px-Harold_Wilson_%281967%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/Harold_Wilson_%281967%29.jpg/340px-Harold_Wilson_%281967%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2021" data-file-height="2695" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Harold_Wilson" title="Harold Wilson">Harold Wilson</a>, Leader of the Opposition and Douglas-Home's successor</figcaption></figure>
<p>The term of the Parliament elected in 1959 was due to expire in October 1964. Parliament was dissolved on 25 September and after three weeks of campaigning the <a href="/wiki/1964_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1964 United Kingdom general election">1964 United Kingdom general election</a> took place on 15 October. Douglas-Home's speeches dealt with the future of the nuclear deterrent, while fears of Britain's relative decline in the world, reflected in chronic balance of payment problems, helped the Labour Party's case.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEYoung2007_150-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung2007-150">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> The Conservatives under Douglas-Home did much better than widely predicted, but Labour under Wilson won with a narrow majority. Labour won 317 seats, the Conservatives 304 and the Liberals 9.<sup id="cite_ref-151" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-151">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="In_Opposition_.281964.E2.80.931970.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="In_Opposition_(1964–1970)">In Opposition (1964–1970)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alec_Douglas-Home&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: In Opposition (1964–1970)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>As Leader of the Opposition, Douglas-Home persuaded Macleod and Powell to rejoin the Conservative front bench. Within weeks of the general election Butler retired from politics, accepting the post of Master of <a href="/wiki/Trinity_College,_Cambridge" title="Trinity College, Cambridge">Trinity College, Cambridge</a> together with a life peerage.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997376_152-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997376-152">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> Douglas-Home did not immediately allocate <a href="/wiki/Shadow_Cabinet" class="mw-redirect" title="Shadow Cabinet">shadow</a> portfolios to his colleagues, but in January 1965 he gave Maudling the foreign affairs brief and Heath became spokesman on Treasury and economic affairs.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997378_153-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997378-153">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> There was no immediate pressure for Douglas-Home to hand over the leadership to a member of the younger generation, but by early 1965 a new Conservative group called PEST (Pressure for Economic and Social Toryism) had discreetly begun to call for a change.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERoth1972180_154-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERoth1972180-154">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> Douglas-Home either did not know, or chose to ignore, the fact that Heath had made a donation to PEST. He decided that the time was coming for him to retire as leader, with Heath as his preferred successor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERoth1972180–182_155-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERoth1972180–182-155">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Enoch_Powell_Allan_Warren.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="An elderly man with full head of hair and small moustache, staring into the camera" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/Enoch_Powell_Allan_Warren.jpg/170px-Enoch_Powell_Allan_Warren.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="236" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/Enoch_Powell_Allan_Warren.jpg/255px-Enoch_Powell_Allan_Warren.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/Enoch_Powell_Allan_Warren.jpg/340px-Enoch_Powell_Allan_Warren.jpg 2x" data-file-width="9445" data-file-height="13094" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Enoch_Powell" title="Enoch Powell">Enoch Powell</a> returned to the Conservative front bench in 1964 and later sought the party leadership.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Determined that the party should abandon the "customary processes of consultation", which had caused such rancour when he was appointed in 1963, Douglas-Home set up an orderly process of secret balloting by Conservative MPs for the election of his immediate and future successors as party leader. In the interests of impartiality the ballot was organised by the <a href="/wiki/1922_Committee" title="1922 Committee">1922 Committee</a>, the backbench Conservative MPs.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERoth1972184–185_156-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERoth1972184–185-156">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup>  Douglas-Home announced his resignation as Conservative leader on 22 July 1965. Three candidates stood in the <a href="/wiki/1965_Conservative_Party_leadership_election" title="1965 Conservative Party leadership election">1965 Conservative Party leadership election</a>: Heath, Maudling and Powell. Heath won with 150 votes (one of them cast by Douglas-Home) to 133 for Maudling and 15 for Powell.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERoth1972186_157-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERoth1972186-157">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Douglas-Home accepted the foreign affairs portfolio in Heath's shadow cabinet. Many expected this to be a short-lived appointment, a prelude to Douglas-Home's retirement from politics.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997392_158-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997392-158">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> It came at a difficult time in British foreign relations: events in the <a href="/wiki/Self-governing_colony" title="Self-governing colony">self-governing colony</a> of <a href="/wiki/Rhodesia" title="Rhodesia">Rhodesia</a> (formerly Southern Rhodesia), which had been drifting towards crisis for some years, finally erupted into open rebellion against British sovereignty. The predominantly white minority government there opposed an immediate transfer to black majority rule before the colony had achieved sovereign statehood, and in November 1965 it <a href="/wiki/Rhodesia%27s_Unilateral_Declaration_of_Independence" title="Rhodesia&#39;s Unilateral Declaration of Independence">unilaterally declared</a> independence. Douglas-Home won the approval of left-wing Labour MPs such as <a href="/wiki/Tony_Benn" title="Tony Benn">Wedgwood Benn</a> for his unwavering opposition to the rebel government, and for ignoring those on the right wing of the Conservative party who sympathised with the rebels on racial grounds.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997392_158-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997392-158">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1966 Douglas-Home became president of the <a href="/wiki/Marylebone_Cricket_Club" title="Marylebone Cricket Club">Marylebone Cricket Club</a> (MCC), which was then the governing body of English and world cricket. The presidency had generally been a largely ceremonial position, but Douglas-Home became embroiled in two controversies, one of them with international implications.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997396–399_159-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997396–399-159">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup>  This was the so-called "<a href="/wiki/D%27Oliveira_affair" title="D&#39;Oliveira affair">D'Oliveira affair</a>", in which the inclusion of a non-white player in the England team to tour South Africa led to the cancellation of the tour by the <a href="/wiki/Apartheid" title="Apartheid">apartheid</a> regime in <a href="/wiki/Pretoria" title="Pretoria">Pretoria</a>. In his account of the affair, the political journalist <a href="/wiki/Peter_Oborne" title="Peter Oborne">Peter Oborne</a> criticises Douglas-Home for his vacillating attitude towards South African Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/John_Vorster" title="John Vorster">John Vorster</a> with whom, says Oborne, "he was no more robust than Chamberlain had been with Hitler thirty years earlier".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOborne2004138–139_160-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOborne2004138–139-160">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> Douglas-Home's advice to the MCC committee not to press the South Africans for advance assurances on D'Oliveira's acceptability, and his optimistic assurances that all would be well, became a matter of much criticism from a group of MCC members led by <a href="/wiki/David_Sheppard" title="David Sheppard">the Rev David Sheppard</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997396–399_159-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997396–399-159">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup> The second controversy was not one of race but of social class. <a href="/wiki/Brian_Close" title="Brian Close">Brian Close</a> was dropped as England captain in favour of <a href="/wiki/Colin_Cowdrey" title="Colin Cowdrey">Colin Cowdrey</a>. Close was dropped after using delaying tactics when captaining <a href="/wiki/Yorkshire_County_Cricket_Club" title="Yorkshire County Cricket Club">Yorkshire</a> in a county match, but the move was widely seen as biased towards cricketers from the old amateur tradition,<sup id="cite_ref-reyburn_161-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-reyburn-161">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> which had officially ended in 1963.<sup id="cite_ref-165" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-165">&#91;n 13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wilson's small majority after the 1964 general election had made the transaction of government business difficult, and in 1966 he called another election in which Labour gained a strong working majority of 96. Some older members of Heath's team, including Lloyd, retired from the front bench, making room for members of the next generation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997393_166-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997393-166">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> Heath moved Maudling to the foreign affairs portfolio, and Douglas-Home took over Lloyd's responsibilities as spokesman on Commonwealth relations.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997393_166-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997393-166">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> Heath was widely seen as ineffective against Wilson, and as the <a href="/wiki/1970_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1970 United Kingdom general election">1970 general election</a> approached there was concern within the party that he would lose, and that Powell would seek to replace him as leader. Maudling and the chief whip, <a href="/wiki/William_Whitelaw" class="mw-redirect" title="William Whitelaw">William Whitelaw</a>, believed that if Heath had to resign Douglas-Home would be the safest candidate to keep Powell out.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997402–03_167-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997402–03-167">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> Douglas-Home shared their view that Labour would win the 1970 election, and that Heath might then have to resign, but he declined to commit himself. To the surprise of almost everyone except Heath, the Conservatives won the election, with a majority of 31 seats.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERoth1972209_168-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERoth1972209-168">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Douglas-Home received an <a href="/wiki/Honorary_degree" title="Honorary degree">Honorary Doctorate</a> from <a href="/wiki/Heriot-Watt_University" title="Heriot-Watt University">Heriot-Watt University</a> in 1966.<sup id="cite_ref-169" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-169">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Foreign_and_Commonwealth_Secretary_.281970.E2.80.931974.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_and_Commonwealth_Secretary_(1970–1974)">Foreign and Commonwealth Secretary (1970–1974)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alec_Douglas-Home&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Foreign and Commonwealth Secretary (1970–1974)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Gorton_and_Alec_Douglas-Home.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/John_Gorton_and_Alec_Douglas-Home.jpg/220px-John_Gorton_and_Alec_Douglas-Home.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="261" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/John_Gorton_and_Alec_Douglas-Home.jpg/330px-John_Gorton_and_Alec_Douglas-Home.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/John_Gorton_and_Alec_Douglas-Home.jpg/440px-John_Gorton_and_Alec_Douglas-Home.jpg 2x" data-file-width="564" data-file-height="669" /></a><figcaption>Douglas-Home with Australian Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/John_Gorton" title="John Gorton">John Gorton</a> in 1970.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Heath invited Douglas-Home to join the cabinet, taking charge of Foreign and Commonwealth Affairs. In earlier centuries it had not been exceptional for a former prime minister to serve in the cabinet of a successor, and even in the previous fifty years <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Balfour" title="Arthur Balfour">Arthur Balfour</a>, Stanley Baldwin, <a href="/wiki/Ramsay_MacDonald" title="Ramsay MacDonald">Ramsay MacDonald</a> and Neville Chamberlain had done so.<sup id="cite_ref-177" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-177">&#91;n 14&#93;</a></sup> As of 2023, Douglas-Home is the last former premier to have served under a successor.<sup id="cite_ref-178" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-178">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup> Of Balfour's appointment to <a href="/wiki/H._H._Asquith" title="H. H. Asquith">Asquith</a>'s cabinet in 1916, <a href="/wiki/Archibald_Primrose,_5th_Earl_of_Rosebery" title="Archibald Primrose, 5th Earl of Rosebery">Lord Rosebery</a>, who had been prime minister in 1894–95, said that having an ex-premier in the cabinet was "a fleeting and dangerous luxury".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997404_179-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997404-179">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup> Thorpe writes that Heath's appointment of Douglas-Home "was not a luxury but an essential buttress to his administration".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997404_179-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997404-179">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Wilson administration had merged the Colonial Office and the Commonwealth Relations Office in 1966 into the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Office" class="mw-redirect" title="Commonwealth Office">Commonwealth Office</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-180" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-180">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup> which, two years later, was merged with the Foreign Office, to form the <a href="/wiki/Foreign_and_Commonwealth_Office" class="mw-redirect" title="Foreign and Commonwealth Office">Foreign and Commonwealth Office</a> (FCO).<sup id="cite_ref-181" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-181">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup> Heath appointed Douglas-Home to head the department, with, once again, a second cabinet minister, this time <a href="/wiki/Anthony_Barber" title="Anthony Barber">Anthony Barber</a>, principally responsible, as Heath had been in the 1960s, for negotiations on Britain's joining the EEC. This time, both ministers were in the Commons; Barber's cabinet post was officially <a href="/wiki/Chancellor_of_the_Duchy_of_Lancaster" title="Chancellor of the Duchy of Lancaster">Chancellor of the Duchy of Lancaster</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-182" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-182">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Within weeks of the election Barber was moved from the FCO to the Treasury to take over as Chancellor from Iain Macleod, who died suddenly on 20 July. Though they had never enjoyed an easy relationship, Douglas-Home recognised his colleague's stature, and felt his loss politically as well as personally.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997405_183-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997405-183">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup> Some commentators have maintained that Macleod's death and replacement by the less substantial figure of Barber fatally undermined the economic success of the Heath government.<sup id="cite_ref-184" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-184">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Barber was replaced at the FCO by <a href="/wiki/Geoffrey_Rippon" title="Geoffrey Rippon">Geoffrey Rippon</a>, who handled the day-to-day negotiations, under the direction of Heath. Douglas-Home, as before, concentrated on east–west and Commonwealth matters. He was in agreement with Heath's policy on the EEC, and did much to persuade doubters on the right wing of the Conservative party of the desirability of Britain's entry. Hurd writes:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>By temperament and background he was some distance removed from Heath's passionate commitment to a united Europe. All the more important was his steadfast support for British entry, which he based on a clear assessment of Britain's place in the modern world, and in particular her relationship with France and Germany on the one hand and the United States on the other&#160;... thus providing the right of the Conservative Party with much needed assurance.<sup id="cite_ref-dnb_30-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dnb-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Andrei_Gromyko_1972_(cropped).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="middle aged man, clean shaven with full head of dark hair" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/Andrei_Gromyko_1972_%28cropped%29.jpg/170px-Andrei_Gromyko_1972_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="232" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/Andrei_Gromyko_1972_%28cropped%29.jpg/255px-Andrei_Gromyko_1972_%28cropped%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/Andrei_Gromyko_1972_%28cropped%29.jpg/340px-Andrei_Gromyko_1972_%28cropped%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="659" data-file-height="900" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Andrei_Gromyko" title="Andrei Gromyko">Andrei Gromyko</a>, Douglas-Home's Soviet counterpart</figcaption></figure>
<p>In east–west relations, Douglas-Home continued his policy of keeping the Soviet Union at bay. In September 1971, after receiving no satisfactory results from negotiations with Gromyko about the flagrant activities of the <a href="/wiki/KGB" title="KGB">KGB</a> in Britain, he expelled 105 Soviet diplomats for spying.<sup id="cite_ref-thaw_185-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-thaw-185">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup> In addition to the furore arising from this,<sup id="cite_ref-leapman_186-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-leapman-186">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup> the Soviets felt that the British government's approach to negotiations on détente in Europe was over-cautious, even sceptical.<sup id="cite_ref-thaw_185-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-thaw-185">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup> Gromyko was nonetheless realistic enough to maintain a working relationship with the British government.<sup id="cite_ref-leapman_186-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-leapman-186">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup> Within days of the expulsions from London he and Douglas-Home met and discussed the Middle East and disarmament.<sup id="cite_ref-leapman_186-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-leapman-186">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup> In this sphere of foreign policy, Douglas-Home was widely judged a success.<sup id="cite_ref-thaw_185-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-thaw-185">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In negotiations on the future of Rhodesia Douglas-Home was less successful. He was instrumental in persuading the rebel leader, <a href="/wiki/Ian_Smith" title="Ian Smith">Ian Smith</a>, to accept proposals for a transition to African majority rule.<sup id="cite_ref-187" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-187">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup> Douglas-Home set up an independent commission chaired by a senior British judge, <a href="/wiki/Edward_Pearce,_Baron_Pearce" title="Edward Pearce, Baron Pearce">Lord Pearce</a>, to investigate how acceptable the proposals were to majority opinion in Rhodesia. After extensive fieldwork throughout Rhodesia, the commission reported, "We are satisfied on our evidence that the proposals are acceptable to the great majority of Europeans. We are equally satisfied&#160;... that the majority of Africans rejected the proposals. In our opinion the people of Rhodesia as a whole do not regard the proposals as acceptable as a basis for independence."<sup id="cite_ref-188" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-188">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> To Douglas-Home's disappointment there was no resolution, and Rhodesia remained a rebel regime long after he left office.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997428_189-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997428-189">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Retirement_.281974.E2.80.931995.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Retirement_(1974–1995)">Retirement (1974–1995)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alec_Douglas-Home&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Retirement (1974–1995)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Alec_Douglas-Home,_by_Allan_Warren.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="head and shoulders image of clean shaven, slim, bald man of mature years; the same man in the picture at the head of the page" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/55/Alec_Douglas-Home%2C_by_Allan_Warren.jpg/220px-Alec_Douglas-Home%2C_by_Allan_Warren.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/55/Alec_Douglas-Home%2C_by_Allan_Warren.jpg/330px-Alec_Douglas-Home%2C_by_Allan_Warren.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/55/Alec_Douglas-Home%2C_by_Allan_Warren.jpg/440px-Alec_Douglas-Home%2C_by_Allan_Warren.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5987" data-file-height="4490" /></a><figcaption>Home, photographed by <a href="/wiki/Allan_Warren" title="Allan Warren">Allan Warren</a>, in 1986</figcaption></figure>
<p>At the <a href="/wiki/February_1974_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="February 1974 United Kingdom general election">February 1974 general election</a> the Heath government was narrowly defeated. Douglas-Home, then aged 70, stepped down at the <a href="/wiki/October_1974_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="October 1974 United Kingdom general election">second election</a> of that year, called in October by the minority Labour government in the hope of winning a working majority. He returned to the House of Lords at the end of 1974 when he accepted a <a href="/wiki/Life_peer" title="Life peer">life peerage</a>, becoming known as <i><b>Baron Home of <a href="/wiki/The_Hirsel" title="The Hirsel">the Hirsel</a></b>, of <a href="/wiki/Coldstream" title="Coldstream">Coldstream</a> in the <a href="/wiki/County_of_Berwick" class="mw-redirect" title="County of Berwick">County of Berwick</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-190" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-190">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Between 1977 and 1989 Home was Governor of <a href="/wiki/I_Zingari" title="I Zingari">I Zingari</a>, the nomadic cricket team.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997463_191-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997463-191">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> In retirement he published three books:  <i>The Way The Wind Blows</i> (1976), described by Hurd as "a good-natured autobiography, with perhaps more anecdotes than insights", <i>Border Reflections</i> (1979),<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHome1979&#39;&#39;&#91;&#91;passim&#93;&#93;&#39;&#39;_192-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHome1979&#39;&#39;[[passim]]&#39;&#39;-192">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup> and his correspondence with his grandson <a href="/wiki/Matthew_Darby" title="Matthew Darby">Matthew Darby</a>, <i>Letters to a Grandson</i> (1983).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHome1983&#39;&#39;passim&#39;&#39;_193-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHome1983&#39;&#39;passim&#39;&#39;-193">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup> In the 1980s Home increasingly spent his time in Scotland, with his family. He was a keen fisherman and enjoyed shooting. Hurd writes that "there was no sudden moment when he abandoned politics", rather that "his interventions became fewer and fewer".<sup id="cite_ref-dnb_30-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dnb-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> His last speech in the House of Lords was in 1989, when he spoke against Hurd's proposals for prosecuting war criminals living in Britain: "After such a lapse of time justice might not be seen to be done. It would be dangerous to rely on memories of events that occurred so long ago. It was too late to reopen the issue."<sup id="cite_ref-194" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-194">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup>  His withdrawal from public affairs became more marked after the death of his wife in 1990, after 54 years of marriage.<sup id="cite_ref-dnb_30-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dnb-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_life">Personal life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alec_Douglas-Home&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Personal life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>In 1936 Douglas-Home married <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_Douglas-Home,_Baroness_Home_of_the_Hirsel" class="mw-redirect" title="Elizabeth Douglas-Home, Baroness Home of the Hirsel">Elizabeth Alington</a>; her father, <a href="/wiki/Cyril_Alington" title="Cyril Alington">Cyril Alington</a>, had been Douglas' headmaster at Eton, and was from 1933 <a href="/wiki/Dean_of_Durham" title="Dean of Durham">Dean of Durham</a>. The service was at <a href="/wiki/Durham_Cathedral" title="Durham Cathedral">Durham Cathedral</a>, conducted by Alington together with <a href="/wiki/William_Temple_(bishop)" title="William Temple (bishop)">William Temple</a>, <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_York" title="Archbishop of York">Archbishop of York</a> and <a href="/wiki/Hensley_Henson" title="Hensley Henson">Hensley Henson</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Durham" title="Bishop of Durham">Bishop of Durham</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-times36_195-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-times36-195">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup> In addition to the large number of aristocratic guests, the household and estate staffs of the Douglas-Home properties at <a href="/wiki/Douglas_Castle" title="Douglas Castle">Douglas Castle</a> and <a href="/wiki/The_Hirsel" title="The Hirsel">the Hirsel</a> were invited.<sup id="cite_ref-times36_195-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-times36-195">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup> There were four children of the marriage: Caroline, Meriel, Diana and <a href="/wiki/David_Douglas-Home,_15th_Earl_of_Home" title="David Douglas-Home, 15th Earl of Home">David</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-timesobit_2-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-timesobit-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> The latter was Home's heir, who became the 15th Earl of Home in 1995.<sup id="cite_ref-197" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-197">&#91;n 15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Douglas-Home died at the Hirsel in October 1995 when he was 92, four months after the death of his parliamentary opponent <a href="/wiki/Harold_Wilson" title="Harold Wilson">Harold Wilson</a>. Home was buried in Lennel churchyard, Coldstream.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997463–464_198-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997463–464-198">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Reputation">Reputation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alec_Douglas-Home&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Reputation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Sir_Alec_Douglas-Home_(geograph_3131225).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/Sir_Alec_Douglas-Home_%28geograph_3131225%29.jpg/220px-Sir_Alec_Douglas-Home_%28geograph_3131225%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/Sir_Alec_Douglas-Home_%28geograph_3131225%29.jpg/330px-Sir_Alec_Douglas-Home_%28geograph_3131225%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/Sir_Alec_Douglas-Home_%28geograph_3131225%29.jpg/440px-Sir_Alec_Douglas-Home_%28geograph_3131225%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3216" data-file-height="4288" /></a><figcaption>Statue of Douglas-Home at <a href="/wiki/The_Hirsel" title="The Hirsel">The Hirsel</a> by sculptor Professor Bill Scott, unveiled in 1998.<sup id="cite_ref-199" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-199">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>Home's premiership was short and not conspicuous for radical innovation. Hurd remarks, "He was not capable of Macmillan's flights of imagination", but he was an effective practical politician.<sup id="cite_ref-dnb_30-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dnb-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> At the Commonwealth Relations Office and the Foreign Office he played an important role in helping to manage Britain's transition from imperial power to European partner. Both Thorpe and Hurd quote a memo that Macmillan wrote in 1963, intended to help the Queen choose his successor:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Lord Home is clearly a man who represents the old governing class at its best&#160;... He is not ambitious in the sense of wanting to scheme for power, although not foolish enough to resist honour when it comes to him&#160;... He gives that impression by a curious mixture of great courtesy, and even if yielding to pressure, with underlying rigidity on matters of principle. It is interesting that he has proved himself so much liked by men like President Kennedy and Mr Rusk and Mr Gromyko. This is exactly the quality that the class to which he belongs have at their best because they think about the question under discussion and not about themselves.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997301_200-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997301-200">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup> </p></blockquote>
<p>Douglas Hurd, once Home's private secretary, and many years later his successor (after seven intermediate holders of the post) as Foreign and Commonwealth Secretary, wrote this personal comment: "The three most courteous men I knew in politics were Lord Home, <a href="/wiki/King_Hussein_of_Jordan" class="mw-redirect" title="King Hussein of Jordan">King Hussein of Jordan</a>, and President <a href="/wiki/Nelson_Mandela" title="Nelson Mandela">Nelson Mandela</a>. All three had ease of birth, in the sense that they never needed to worry about who they themselves were and so had more time to concern themselves with the feelings of others."<sup id="cite_ref-dnb_30-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dnb-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although some in the Conservative party agreed with Wilson (and <a href="/wiki/Jo_Grimond" title="Jo Grimond">Jo Grimond</a>, the Liberal leader) that the Conservatives would have won the 1964 election if Butler had been prime minister, <i>The Times</i> observed, "it should not be overlooked that in October 1963 Home took over a Government whose morale was shattered and whose standing in the opinion polls was abysmal. A year later Labour won the general election, with an overall majority of only four seats. That [Home] recovered so much ground in so short a time was in itself an achievement." Looking back across Home's career, <i>The Times</i> considered that his reputation rested not on his brief premiership, but on his two spells as Foreign Secretary: "He brought to the office&#160;... his capacity for straight talking, for toughness towards the Soviet Union and for firmness (sometimes interpreted as a lack of sympathy) towards the countries of Africa and Asia. But he brought something else as well: an unusual degree of international respect."<sup id="cite_ref-timesobit_2-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-timesobit-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Cabinet_.281963.E2.80.931964.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Cabinet_(1963–1964)">Cabinet (1963–1964)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alec_Douglas-Home&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Cabinet (1963–1964)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The Home cabinet, announced on 20 October 1963, was:<sup id="cite_ref-201" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-201">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li>Lord Home (Sir Alec Douglas-Home from 23 October): Prime Minister and First Lord of the Treasury</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Rab_Butler" title="Rab Butler">R A Butler</a>: <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Foreign_Affairs_(UK)" class="mw-redirect" title="Secretary of State for Foreign Affairs (UK)">Secretary of State for Foreign Affairs</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Quintin_Hogg,_Baron_Hailsham_of_St_Marylebone" title="Quintin Hogg, Baron Hailsham of St Marylebone">Quintin Hogg</a>: <a href="/wiki/Lord_President_of_the_Council" title="Lord President of the Council">Lord President of the Council</a> and Minister for Science</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Reginald_Manningham-Buller,_1st_Viscount_Dilhorne" title="Reginald Manningham-Buller, 1st Viscount Dilhorne">Lord Dilhorne</a>: <a href="/wiki/Lord_Chancellor" title="Lord Chancellor">Lord Chancellor</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Reginald_Maudling" title="Reginald Maudling">Reginald Maudling</a>: <a href="/wiki/Chancellor_of_the_Exchequer" title="Chancellor of the Exchequer">Chancellor of the Exchequer</a><sup id="cite_ref-202" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-202">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Henry_Brooke,_Baron_Brooke_of_Cumnor" title="Henry Brooke, Baron Brooke of Cumnor">Henry Brooke</a>: <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_the_Home_Department" class="mw-redirect" title="Secretary of State for the Home Department">Secretary of State for the Home Department</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Duncan_Sandys" title="Duncan Sandys">Duncan Sandys</a>: <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_the_Colonies" title="Secretary of State for the Colonies">Secretary of State for the Colonies</a> and <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Commonwealth_Relations" title="Secretary of State for Commonwealth Relations">Secretary of State for Commonwealth Relations</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Edward_Heath" title="Edward Heath">Edward Heath</a>: <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Industry,_Trade,_and_Regional_Development" class="mw-redirect" title="Secretary of State for Industry, Trade, and Regional Development">Secretary of State for Industry, Trade, and Regional Development</a> and <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_Board_of_Trade" title="President of the Board of Trade">President of the Board of Trade</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Peter_Thorneycroft" title="Peter Thorneycroft">Peter Thorneycroft</a>: <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Defence" title="Secretary of State for Defence">Minister of Defence</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Selwyn_Lloyd" title="Selwyn Lloyd">Selwyn Lloyd</a>: <a href="/wiki/Lord_Privy_Seal" title="Lord Privy Seal">Lord Privy Seal</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/John_Hare,_1st_Viscount_Blakenham" title="John Hare, 1st Viscount Blakenham">Lord Blakenham</a>: <a href="/wiki/Chancellor_of_the_Duchy_of_Lancaster" title="Chancellor of the Duchy of Lancaster">Chancellor of the Duchy of Lancaster</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Christopher_Soames,_Baron_Soames" class="mw-redirect" title="Christopher Soames, Baron Soames">Christopher Soames</a>: <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Agriculture,_Fisheries_and_Food" title="Minister of Agriculture, Fisheries and Food">Minister of Agriculture, Fisheries and Food</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Ernest_Marples" title="Ernest Marples">Ernest Marples</a>: <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Transport" title="Secretary of State for Transport">Minister of Transport</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/John_Boyd-Carpenter" class="mw-redirect" title="John Boyd-Carpenter">John Boyd-Carpenter</a>: <a href="/wiki/Chief_Secretary_to_the_Treasury" title="Chief Secretary to the Treasury">Chief Secretary to the Treasury</a> and <a href="/wiki/Paymaster_General" title="Paymaster General">Paymaster General</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Michael_Noble,_Baron_Glenkinglas" title="Michael Noble, Baron Glenkinglas">Michael Noble</a>: <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Scotland" title="Secretary of State for Scotland">Secretary of State for Scotland</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Edward_Boyle,_Baron_Boyle_of_Handsworth" title="Edward Boyle, Baron Boyle of Handsworth">Sir Edward Boyle</a>: <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Education_and_Science" class="mw-redirect" title="Secretary of State for Education and Science">Minister of Education</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Joseph_Godber" title="Joseph Godber">Joseph Godber</a>: <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Labour_(United_Kingdom)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of Labour (United Kingdom)">Minister of Labour</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Keith_Joseph" title="Keith Joseph">Sir Keith Joseph</a>: <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Housing_and_Local_Government" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of Housing and Local Government">Minister of Housing and Local Government</a> and Minister for Welsh Affairs</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Frederick_Erroll,_1st_Baron_Erroll_of_Hale" title="Frederick Erroll, 1st Baron Erroll of Hale">Frederick Erroll</a>: <a href="/wiki/Ministry_of_Power_(United_Kingdom)" title="Ministry of Power (United Kingdom)">Minister of Power</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Anthony_Barber" title="Anthony Barber">Anthony Barber</a>: <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Health" class="mw-redirect" title="Secretary of State for Health">Minister of Health</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Geoffrey_Rippon" title="Geoffrey Rippon">Geoffrey Rippon</a>: <a href="/wiki/Ministry_of_Works_(United_Kingdom)" title="Ministry of Works (United Kingdom)">Minister of Public Building and Works</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Bill_Deedes" title="Bill Deedes">W F Deedes</a>: <a href="/wiki/Minister_without_portfolio_(United_Kingdom)" title="Minister without portfolio (United Kingdom)">Minister without Portfolio</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Peter_Carington,_6th_Baron_Carrington" title="Peter Carington, 6th Baron Carrington">Lord Carrington</a>: Minister without Portfolio, <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_House_of_Lords" title="Leader of the House of Lords">Leader of the House of Lords</a></li></ul>
<dl><dt>Changes</dt></dl>
<ul><li>April 1964: Quintin Hogg became Secretary of State for Education and Science. Sir Edward Boyle left the cabinet. The post of Minister of Defence became <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Defence" title="Secretary of State for Defence">Secretary of State for Defence</a> with Thorneycroft retaining it.</li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Arms">Arms</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alec_Douglas-Home&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Arms">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<table class="wikitable mw-collapsible" style="max-width: 100%">
<caption class="nowrap">Coat of arms of Alec Douglas-Home
</caption>
<tbody><tr>
<td style="width: 220px;"><span class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Frameless"><a href="/wiki/File:Coat_of_Arms_of_Alec_Douglas-Home,_14th_Earl_of_Home.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Coat_of_Arms_of_Alec_Douglas-Home%2C_14th_Earl_of_Home.svg/220px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Alec_Douglas-Home%2C_14th_Earl_of_Home.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="242" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Coat_of_Arms_of_Alec_Douglas-Home%2C_14th_Earl_of_Home.svg/330px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Alec_Douglas-Home%2C_14th_Earl_of_Home.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Coat_of_Arms_of_Alec_Douglas-Home%2C_14th_Earl_of_Home.svg/440px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Alec_Douglas-Home%2C_14th_Earl_of_Home.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1403" data-file-height="1542" /></a></span>
</td>
<td style="min-width:175px;">
<dl><dt>Notes</dt>
<dd>The image at left shows his <a href="/wiki/Achievement_(heraldry)" title="Achievement (heraldry)">achievement</a> of arms as <a href="/wiki/Earl_of_Home" title="Earl of Home">Earl of Home</a>.</dd>
<dt>Crest</dt>
<dd>1st, On a Cap of Maintenance Proper a Lion's Head erased Argent (Home); 2nd, On a Cap of Maintenance Proper a Salamander Vert encircled with Flames of Fire Proper (Douglas).</dd>
<dt>Escutcheon</dt>
<dd>Quarterly, 1st and 4th grandquarterly, 1st and 4th, Vert a Lion rampant Argent armed and langued Gules (Home); 2nd and 3rd, Argent three Popinjays Vert beaked and membered Gules (Pepdie of Dunglas); over all an Inescutcheon Or charged with an Orle Azure (Landale); 2nd and 3rd grandquarterly, 1st, Azure a Lion rampant Argent armed and langued Gules crowned with an Imperial Crown Or (Lordship of Galloway); 2nd, Or a Lion rampant Gules armed and langued Azure debruised of a Ribbon Sable (Abernethy); 3rd, Argent three Piles Gules (Lordship of Brechin); 4th, Or a Fess chequy Azure and Argent surmounted of a Bend Sable charged with three Buckles of the Field (Stewart of Bonkill); over all on an Inescutcheon Argent a Man's Heart Gules ensigned with an Imperial Crown Proper and a Chief Azure charged with three Mullets of the Field (Douglas).</dd>
<dt>Supporters</dt>
<dd>On either side a Lion Argent armed and langued Gules.</dd>
<dt>Motto</dt>
<dd>Above the first crest: A Home, a Home, a Home.<br />Above the second crest: <i>Jamais arrière</i> (Never behind).<br />Below the arms: True to the end.</dd>
<dt>Orders</dt>
<dd>The collar of the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_Thistle" title="Order of the Thistle">Order of the Thistle</a>.</dd></dl>
</td></tr></tbody></table>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes_and_references">Notes and references</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alec_Douglas-Home&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Notes and references">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p><b>Notes</b>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist reflist-columns references-column-width" style="column-width: 24em;">
<ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-16"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-16">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">In the same 1963 memorandum, Home revealed more of his individual political philosophy, writing that whereas country people get "pretty close to true values", the rootless townspeople "need constant leadership. It is, however, they who have the votes..." He added: "A large part in my decision [to become PM] was the feeling that only by simple straightforward talk to the industrial masses (sic) could we hope to defeat the Socialists".<sup id="cite_ref-H285_15-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H285-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-37"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-37">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">In a 1964 study of Douglas-Home John Dickie comments that Dunglass as a PPS lacked influence in decision making, and that such opprobrium as later attached to him was "guilt by association".<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Thorpe in his biography of Harold Macmillan writes that Butler's career was blighted by his support for the Munich agreement as a Foreign Office minister, but that <span style="padding-right:.15em;">"</span>'Munich' was never held against Alec Douglas-Home".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThorpe2010135_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThorpe2010135-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-42"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-42">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">According to Thorpe, Douglas-Home was the only British Prime Minister known to have read the work.<sup id="cite_ref-t115_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-t115-41">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-51"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-51">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Labour's majority of five seats was not thought large enough to sustain the party through a full five-year term in office. <a href="/wiki/George_VI" title="George VI">George&#160;VI</a> was due to be absent for six months on a Commonwealth tour, and Attlee agreed that it was necessary that the King should leave behind a stable government not likely to fall in his absence. Attlee called a further election in October 1951 at a time not advantageous to his party, which was lagging behind the Conservatives in opinion polls. Labour polled more votes than the Conservatives at the election, but the British first-past-the-post electoral system nevertheless gave more seats to the Conservatives. The King's tour did not take place because of his poor health.<sup id="cite_ref-ts245_50-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ts245-50">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-72"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-72">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The federation consisted of <a href="/wiki/Northern_Rhodesia" title="Northern Rhodesia">Northern Rhodesia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Southern_Rhodesia" title="Southern Rhodesia">Southern Rhodesia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Nyasaland" title="Nyasaland">Nyasaland</a>. The first and third were still colonies and came under Macleod's purview; Southern Rhodesia, which had self-government, was the responsibility of Home's department.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-87"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-87">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The legality of US actions in the crisis, including the blockade of Cuba, has subsequently been questioned by American writers specialising in law, including <a href="/wiki/Abram_Chayes" title="Abram Chayes">Abram Chayes</a> in <i>The Cuban Missile Crisis: International Crises and the Role of Law</i>, and <a href="/wiki/Stephen_Rosskamm_Shalom" class="mw-redirect" title="Stephen Rosskamm Shalom">Stephen R Shalom</a> in <i>International Lawyers and Other Apologists: The Case of the Cuban Missile Crisis</i>. The former concludes that American actions were not in breach of international law;<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> the latter takes the contrary view.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-96"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-96">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">If Macmillan had resigned a year earlier or a year later, neither Hailsham nor Home could have been candidates for the succession. The <a href="/wiki/Peerage_Act_1963" title="Peerage Act 1963">Peerage Act</a> became law in 1963 after a three-year campaign by <a href="/wiki/Tony_Benn" title="Tony Benn">Anthony Wedgwood Benn</a>, who had reluctantly inherited his father's peerage in 1960.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> Under this law existing peers had twelve months from 31 July 1963 in which they could disclaim their peerages.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-108"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-108">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">In 1980 a biographer of Macmillan, George Hutchinson, expressed strong doubt about the reliability of Dilhorne's figures.<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-124"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-124">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"> The subordinate titles were the lordship of Dunglass, the lordship of Home, the lordship of Hume of Berwick, the barony of Douglas and the barony of Hume of Berwick.<sup id="cite_ref-p463_115-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p463-115">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-128"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-128">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Technically, no Prime Minister, or any other politician, is a Member of Parliament between the dissolution of one Parliament and the election of another,<sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> but Douglas-Home was singular in being a member of neither house while a current Parliament was still in being. Although there was no precedent in modern British Parliamentary history, there were analogous cases in at least one other Commonwealth legislature: <a href="/wiki/William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King" title="William Lyon Mackenzie King">Mackenzie King</a> twice remained as Prime Minister of Canada having lost his seat, in 1925 and 1945, returning to the Canadian House of Commons in by-elections.<sup id="cite_ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-137"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-137">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">This running joke began in 1964 when a provincial newspaper, the <i><a href="/wiki/Aberdeen_Evening_Express" class="mw-redirect" title="Aberdeen Evening Express">Aberdeen Evening Express</a></i> accidentally used a picture of Douglas-Home over a caption referring to a baillie called Vass.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEIngrams1971104_134-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEIngrams1971104-134">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> <i>Private Eye</i> then affected to believe that Douglas-Home was an impostor whom the newspaper had unmasked; the magazine maintained this fiction throughout the rest of Douglas-Home's premiership and thereafter.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997262_135-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThorpe1997262-135">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> <i>Private Eye</i> extended the notion to include Douglas-Home's nephew, the journalist <a href="/wiki/Charles_Douglas-Home_(journalist)" title="Charles Douglas-Home (journalist)">Charles Douglas-Home</a>, whom it dubbed "Charles Vass".<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-149"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-149">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Douglas-Home never publicly spoke of the kidnapping because he did not want to ruin the career of his bodyguard but told the story in 1977 to Hailsham, who recorded it in his diaries.<sup id="cite_ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> In July 2009 <a href="/wiki/BBC_Radio_4" title="BBC Radio 4">BBC Radio 4</a> broadcast a dramatisation of the event entitled <i>The Night They Tried to Kidnap the Prime Minister</i>, written by Martin Jameson and starring <a href="/wiki/Tim_McInnerny" title="Tim McInnerny">Tim McInnerny</a> as Douglas-Home.<sup id="cite_ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-165"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-165">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Until 1963 those taking part in first-class cricket were classed as "Gentlemen" (amateurs) or "Players" (professionals).<sup id="cite_ref-162" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-162">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> Amateurs had long dominated the running of the game. Until 1950 the panel of selectors who chose the England team was exclusively amateur (with the exception of the 1926 and 1930 panels to which <a href="/wiki/Jack_Hobbs" title="Jack Hobbs">Jack Hobbs</a> and <a href="/wiki/Wilfred_Rhodes" title="Wilfred Rhodes">Wilfred Rhodes</a> were co-opted)<sup id="cite_ref-163" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-163">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> and it was not until 1952 that a professional cricketer, <a href="/wiki/Leonard_Hutton" class="mw-redirect" title="Leonard Hutton">Leonard Hutton</a>, was first appointed captain of the England team.<sup id="cite_ref-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-164">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> Close was from the professional side of the game. <i>The Birmingham Post</i> wrote of him, "the man possibly destined to become England's greatest cricket captain, was sacrificed on the altar of the old school tie. In drizzly conditions at Edgbaston in 1967, Yorkshire under Close deprived Warwickshire of victory with timewasting tactics that finally saw just two overs bowled in the last 15 minutes."<sup id="cite_ref-reyburn_161-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-reyburn-161">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-177"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-177">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"> Prime ministers who served under one or more of their successors included: <a href="/wiki/The_Duke_of_Grafton" class="mw-redirect" title="The Duke of Grafton">the Duke of Grafton</a> under <a href="/wiki/Frederick_North,_Lord_North" title="Frederick North, Lord North">North</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPike1968103_170-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPike1968103-170">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/The_Duke_of_Portland" class="mw-redirect" title="The Duke of Portland">the Duke of Portland</a> under <a href="/wiki/William_Pitt_the_Younger" title="William Pitt the Younger">Pitt</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPike1968127_171-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPike1968127-171">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Henry_Addington" title="Henry Addington">Addington</a> under <a href="/wiki/William_Grenville,_1st_Baron_Grenville" title="William Grenville, 1st Baron Grenville">Grenville</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPike1968151_172-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPike1968151-172">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/F._J._Robinson,_1st_Viscount_Goderich" title="F. J. Robinson, 1st Viscount Goderich">Goderich</a> under <a href="/wiki/Charles_Grey,_2nd_Earl_Grey" title="Charles Grey, 2nd Earl Grey">Grey</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPike1968177_173-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPike1968177-173">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington" title="Arthur Wellesley, 1st Duke of Wellington">Wellington</a> under <a href="/wiki/Robert_Peel" title="Robert Peel">Peel</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPike1968184_174-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPike1968184-174">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup> Balfour under <a href="/wiki/Asquith" class="mw-redirect" title="Asquith">Asquith</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lloyd_George" class="mw-redirect" title="Lloyd George">Lloyd George</a> and Baldwin,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPike1968328–29_175-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPike1968328–29-175">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> Baldwin under MacDonald and MacDonald under Baldwin, and Chamberlain under Churchill.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPike1968390_176-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPike1968390-176">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-197"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-197">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Douglas-Home's biographer D. R. Thorpe notes that during the passage through Parliament of the Peerage Act 1963, the draft legislation originally provided that a disclaimed peerage would lapse permanently, rather than merely for the lifetime of the disclaimant. Thorpe observes that if this provision had remained a condition of disclaiming his earldom in 1963, thus preventing his son from inheriting the title in due course, Home would not have gone ahead and would not have become prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-196" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-196">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
</ol></div>

<li id="cite_note-35"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-35">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite class="citation cs2">"Foreign and Comparative Government", <i><a href="/wiki/The_American_Political_Science_Review" class="mw-redirect" title="The American Political Science Review">The American Political Science Review</a></i>, <b>59</b> (1): 184–207, March 1965, <a href="/wiki/Doi_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="Doi (identifier)">doi</a>:<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://doi.org/10.2307%2F1976143">10.2307/1976143</a>, <a href="/wiki/JSTOR_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="JSTOR (identifier)">JSTOR</a>&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.jstor.org/stable/1976143">1976143</a>, <a href="/wiki/S2CID_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="S2CID (identifier)">S2CID</a>&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://api.semanticscholar.org/CorpusID:151984436">151984436</a></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Ajournal&amp;rft.genre=article&amp;rft.jtitle=The+American+Political+Science+Review&amp;rft.atitle=Foreign+and+Comparative+Government&amp;rft.volume=59&amp;rft.issue=1&amp;rft.pages=184-207&amp;rft.date=1965-03&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fapi.semanticscholar.org%2FCorpusID%3A151984436%23id-name%3DS2CID&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.jstor.org%2Fstable%2F1976143%23id-name%3DJSTOR&amp;rft_id=info%3Adoi%2F10.2307%2F1976143&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AAlec+Douglas-Home" class="Z3988"></span> <span style="font-size:0.95em; font-size:95%; color:#555">(subscription required)</span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-FOOTNOTEThorpe2010135-36"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThorpe2010135_36-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFThorpe2010">Thorpe (2010)</a>, p.&#160;135</span>
</li>
